WOMAN'S REALM. DRESS-FASHION-HOME.

WOMEN'S RANGE OF WORK. Girl caddies are mentioned as curiosities on the links at Marienbad, but it is in France and Belgium that one realises how far women's right to work is recognised. In Paris. at the open-aii concerts in the Tuileries Gardens, women take the money at the gates and sell programmes in the enclosure. They also in some of the gar- dens see to the.hiring of the chairs. At country railway stations girls and women do the work ol newsboys, and trundle the refreshment waggon along the platform. On the railways women are employed at level crossings, where they stand holding out a furled flag as the train passes by, ! after the manner of soldiers presenting arms. ! In some of the provincial towns women are em- I ployed as scavengers. Even in towns like Rennes and Antwerp scavenging is done by ! women, who go about with huge besoms sweep- : ing up the street refuse and wheeling away the ! garbage in barrows. The number of carts driven by women is also,noticeable.

The Earl nodded approvingly, and, leaning back, shut his eyes as if the subject were con- cluded; and Talbot, taking a book at random, went out of the .room in his slow and languid fashion. It was not until he had reached the hall that he realised that his courage had failed him, and that he had not ventured to sound tho Earl on his "early life."

A PRETTY SILK BLOUSE.

Quite of a new and original type is the smart little blouse which forms the subject of our sketch. The material employed in its realisation is a heavy brown Shantung silk of rather coarse weave, just the thing for early-autumn wear. This blouse is made in crossover style, the cross- ing fronts being adorned with wide plain straps of the silk, edged on either side with a line of machine-stitching. These straps, after crossing just below the bust, hang loose, and are finished with double-pointed ends, in each of which is set

WOMEN'S CHAT.

Royal Visits and Visitors.—The King is next week to be the guest of Lord and Lady Savile, at Rufford Abbey, and will attend the autumn meeting at Doncaster, which always attracts a large muster of the fashionable world during the second week in September. After Doncaiter, the King will spend three or four weeks in the Highlands, where already many members of the Royal Family are staying. The Prince and Princess of Wales and their children are at Abergeldie, where fishing in the Dee is one of the chief delights of the young Princes. The Princess Royal is at Mar Lodge, with her family, and there are,unbappily,rumours that the health of the Princess is again far from satis- factory, and that a fresh operation may be necessary. The Duke and Duchess of Connaukht, and Princess Patricia have been the guests of the Duke and Duchess of Sutherland at Dunrobin Castle, and Prince Arthur— I sometimes called the " handy-man "of the Royal I Family—has been paying a round of visits in Scotland. Later on in the autumn, the King and Queen will enter- tain several Royal visitors from abroad, including the King and Queen of Norway, and the King and Queen of Spain, also the German Emperor and Empress, who, it it is hoped, will bring with them their only daughter Princess Victoria Louise, now fifteen years of age. Mar Lodge.—The situation of Mar Lodge is ideal, nestling as it does, amid beautiful gardens of protect- ing trees down near the Dee, where the Princess Royal and her daughters spend so much of their time angling for the salmon that abound there. The utmost privacy is secured by the Duke of Fife at Mar Lodge, the tenants and employees on the estate being forbidden, when he is in residence there, to entertain any strangers without express permission. The Indispensable Game.—Bridge, unlike shooting, peg-tops, and marbles, is more or less in season all the year round, but the proper season according to the New Bridge Club, commences with September. It is the only thing the men are fit for after a day's shooting, fishing, or golfing, and in the country after dinner it is mostly a case of bridge or nothing. Far from bridge going out of fashion, it seems to be played more than ever, and is looked upon in country houses as indispensable. A ' Suggested Social Revolution.—Lady Perbright suggests that we should abolish dinner and dinner parties as social entertainments, which, she says, have become as the feast of Belshazzar, and the banquets of I Lucullus. It is an excellent idea for those in training I for the Simple Life, but much of what we heir on this ' subject is from people who are merely playing at it for a while, and from faddists who think a diet of bread, water, and vegetables an excellent thing for their friends. There are, of course, inanypeople in the world who live to eat, and the desire to show off," to have a more expensive chef, and give a grander dinner party Ilarge button of brown Shantung embroidered than our neighbours, is by no means confined to the a with French knots worked in fine goldthread. ostentatious .no wor ne go "vulgar rich." From her husband if be, like a wife, is to share the dignity. the neck turn back revers and collar of plain Old World Fabrics.—lt seems as though many of the sturdy materials so fashionable half a century ago, will find favour during tbe autumn and winter months. Gros grain, perceptibly ribbed, as its name indicates,—'and warranted as in the olden days to "stand alone" is already to the front in the silk departments, and brocades are being utilised for quite young matrons and even debutantes dinner dresses. Again our old friend cashmere is, to a great extent, taking the place of taffetas for afternoon wear, and in white cream and the eery palest shades, it is appearing in the evening. Generally speaking airy-fairy fabrics are to he less in demand; we are to be more "Solid " and though the notion must be more expensive to start with, it will certainly prove more economical in the long run. Colour Schemes,—Those among us who have thoteughly Mastered the combination of two colours, should promptly pass on to the consideration of the third colour-element, which successfully carried out, makes immensely for interest and becomingness. A 1 capital rule to follow in this particular instance is to have the mass of the colour of the costume match the bair, the first accessory the skin, while the second accessory should match the eyes. The girl or woman too who possesses a costume carried out in two colours and finds it not altogether satisfactory, should try the introduction of a third hue, which should be placed if possible near the face, and match the eyes. In the matter of colour schemes we have, during the past few years, made immense strides, and the study is one that will repay us well, provided we take it in hand patiently and seriously. Fascinating Collars.—A new description of jewelled collar for evening wear has made its appearance and this is greatly in request among Society women. 'lt is built in three pieces,naniely the one that extends across the front of tbe neck, and the clasp, and these are strung together with narrow bands of velvet or tulle. Already the notion is being carried out under " imitation " conditions, but so beautiful are the designs, and so artistically are the" gems" set, that the most fastidious will think many times before passing these particular ornaments by.

grey cloth, which forms an excellent contract to the rough grey tweed. The cape which comes over this cloak is cut long enough to hang well below the waist, and is finished with a line of machine-stitching all round the edges. Three large buttons, similar to those which fasten th• cloak, are placed on either side of the front, and form a suitable decoration for this over-cape. Detachable sleeves are provided with this smart cloak, and can be sewn in later in the year, when the colder weather will make the extra warmth very acceptable.

NOT SLAVES TO FASHION:

For every woman who dresses in a picturesqus and individual manner there are some hundreds who wish to and yet have not the talent or in- stinct to do it for thems-es, and here, of course, says a wri ea ter in the :railer, is the oppor- tunity for the dressmaker with ideas; and every season we have a fresh crop of them, so that it really seems as if our taste in future is likely to grow gradually better • again. The younger generation is taking most kindly to the change of thought, and there are now many charming young girls who dress entirely accord- ing to their own fancies, and by whom the dress- maker with her "this is what everybody is wear- ing" finds herself most mercilessly crushed.

TO-DAY'S RECIPE,

HALIBUT A LA MADiam.—Put a nice thick steak of halibut in a 6aucep4ii of cold water with some onion, thyme, parsley, peppercorns, salt, and a little' vinegar. As soon as it simmers draw it to the side of the fire and let it cook gently. When cold servo with the 'following sauce: Pound up in a mortar a hard-boiled egg, loz. of him-4.r. three anchovies, a sprig of chervil. * little tarragon vinegar, a xew capers, gnerkins, and a shallot (finely minced), and work all well f.ogether, adding gradually enough salad oil to make a smooth sauce, then rub it through a sieve and pour it over the fish.

APPLETON RIFLE CLUB.

Monthly Prize Competition.

The fourth of the series of fiva monthly prize com- r 3titions for the present season, was held at the Range on Saturday. The afternoon was a very busy one, over 30 entries being received. The following were the scores, the distance in all cases being 100 yards. BRONZE BADGE CLASS.—Fired with the Servire Rifle: Nine shots at stationary target, 3A inch bull and nine shots at a vanishing figure target.. Possible 90 : Messrs. F. C. Bennion' 65; P. D. Gardiner, 64 ;. J. E. I Hobin, 55: J. S. Shotton, 52; T. I. Fletcher, 47; W. Sherratt, 33. OPEN SWEEPSTAKES.—Any rifle • five shots; stationary target; 3i-inch bull ; possible 25. Messrs. J. E. Hobia, 22 ; F. C. Bennion, 22 ; T. I. Fletcher, 19 ; P. D. Gardiner, 19 •, T. Daintith, 17 ; W. Sherratt, 15 ; J'l S. Shotton, 14; P. Maginn, 14. MEMBERS' STAKES.—Any rifle; seven shots; station- ary target; 6, inch bull. Possible 35. Messrs. T. Daintith, 34; E. Pollard, 33; T. I. Fletcher, 33; S. Halliwell, 33; P. D. Gardiner, 32; W. Sherratt, 32 • .1 A. Booth, 32; P. Maginn, 32; B. Percival, 31; F. C. Bennion, 31; J. E. Hobin, 29; J. Whitfield, 29; J. S. Shotton' 29; J. Towers, 26; R. Thompson, 26. A re-shoot for the second place resulted ma win for )fr. Pollard. TYRO STAKES.—The conditions were s:milar to the members' stakes. Possible, 35. Messrs. J. Whitfield, 34; E. Pollard, 32; I. Broady, 30 ; R. Thompson, 25 ; J. Towers, 24. N.R.A. "RIFLEMAN'S CERTIFICATES.”—For miriature rifles ; 31-inch bull ; stationary target ; distance 100 yards; thirty shots ; possible 150 ; score requirel 114. The following certificates have been awarded during the past month :—Mr. P. D. Gardiner, 134; Sergeant- Major Ingram, 121 ; Messrs. J. E. Hobin, 121; S. 119 ; A. Warburton, 115. The Club season ends on the 28th.of September, when the Donegal Bronze Badge will be shot for. This badge is provided annually by the National Rifle Associatior, The members of the Club were pleased to receive a visit from Viscount Crosse who was present at the Range on the 17th of August, and who appeared to be greatly interested in what was going on.

The Examiner, Saturday, September 7, 1907. RURAL DISTRICT COUNCIL. LOCAL IMPROVEMENTS.

A DAY'S LOCAL GOVERNMENT BOARD INQUIRY.

fact to be mentioned in Domesday Book. Since the Order of 1893 I can say exactly what we have spent. But we commenced in 1881 with the widening of some streets. The Inspector: Has any estimate been found of what the whole of the improvements are going to cost ? The Town Clerk : No. It is impossible to form an estimate. Is it going to cost a million ?—I should say about £200,000 if you want a rough guess. How much of that has already been done? The Borough TN.asurer said about £78,000 had been expended under the Order. Councillor Wilding remarked that the Corporation prescribed a fresh building line nearly every day. (Laughter.) The Inspector: You have spent a quarter of a million already, and it will be getting on for a quarter of a million more before you have done the widening shown on the plan. You do not pledge yourselves to stop there. With traffic and population increasing, other widenings may be necessary. The Town Clerk : £164,000 has been sanctioned since 1806 including Bridge-street. Some of that is included in this scheme and some is not. In answer to the Inspector the Town Clerk said it was impossible to distinguish between the sums expended under the Provisional Order and the Local Act of 1899 respectively. The Inspector: I think it would be possible If I adjourned the inquiry. How long would it take—a w3ek ? The Town Clerk : I would not undertake to do it within a month. The Inspector: Whenever a scheme comes forward in instalments the Board like to know the whole scope of the schema and where we are. 1 Mr. Gough: They don't know themselves, I'm 1

afraid. - Alderman Bennett : It is impossible to get the Council to go in for a really comprehensive scheme. They are not far-sighted and statesmanlike enough to go in for that. The Inspector : They don't seem to be able to say what the scope of their scheme is, but they want .ClO,OOO for it. The Town Clerk said the Corporation had acquired all the property in Bridge-street and they were now proceeding with Buttermarket-street. Councillor Wilding : The officials have been given authority by the Council to go to the Local Govern- ment Board long before they did. The Inspector: You must allow them some latitude in nicking up pieces of property. The Town Clerk said that more than eighteen months ago he placed a list before the General Purposes Com- mittee and asked for leave to apply to the Local Government Board. It was only recently that they instructed him to do so. Councillor Pemberton : It would only be fair to -, give the reason why we did not apply. I The Town Clerk: 1 cannot go into your reasons.

_ The purchases were then disc-ussed seriatim. Councillor Lord said some of the properties were bought by a small committee without the knowledge of the Council. The Town Clerk : They have all been before the Council. _

Councillor Lord : At some time or other. Councillor Pemberton: Some of these purchases have never been before the Couucil.

The Town Clerk: I say they have all been. Councillor Pemberton: They have not been on the minutes.

In the case of a plot of land at the corner of Church- street and Howley Lane the Town Clerk admitted that the Corporation had" paid through the nose" .for it, but they had to abide by the award of the arbitrator. The question of purchasing property again cropped up, and the Town Clerk said the opposition suggested that the Corporation should stand on the house tops and prolllaim that they were going to buy property by public auction. Councillor Pemberton t If you have a prescribed line it is obvious to anycke that the Council will buy the property they require; but there ought to be the consent of the Council. He added that the Council had abolished the Property Purchasing Committee and could now exercise a tighter hand. There was considerable discussion with regard to the Mersey Works purchased from Mr. J. A. Edelsten at a cost of £8,291 2s. 3d. 'rhe Inspector said the cost was about £4 per yard. Mr. Barritt : Isn't this the property which caused a great deal of unpleasantness—about the unwise purchase the Corporation made? The Town Clerk: Some people thought so. Was not there an action to compel the Corporation to carry their contract out ?—There was. It was the feeling of the members that the Corpora- tion bad made a very unwise purchase?—Some members thought so. Councillor Pemberton : The majority did. The Town Clerk : At one time; but they changed their opinion afterwards. Councillor Pemberton: When they were involved in a law suit they had no choice.

In answer to Mr. Gough, The Town Clerk said he was present at the negotia- tions and the owner never promised to rebuild or to re-instate, but he expressed his desire to do so and since that date be re-instated his business and was carrying it on in Thynne-street. He had not displaced a single workman. The improvement was an important one as Knutsford-road was one of the main highways from,Cheshire to Lancashire. Mr. Barritt asked if the price did not compare very unfavourably with that paid for land lower down the same road?

The Town Clerk said the latter was a very good purchase but the Corporation simply bought the land and had no business to acquire, as the concern was in Liquidation. It was very unfair to compare the two. Mr. Gough asked why, if there was a difference between the estimates of the committee and Mr. Edelsten, the matter was not submitted to arbitration in the intetests of the ratepayers. The Town Clerk: Because we agreed to terms. We had two conferences with Mr. Edelsten and at the second we came to terms. It would be useless and foolish to waste money in arbitration proceedings. We have made some good bargains and we may have :been pinched in one or two. In my opinion we would have paid more for the Mersey Works by arbitration, and the Borough Surveyor bears me out in that opinion. Councillor Pemberton: This is one of the largest purchases made and it was done in a most secret manner. The Town Clerk: That is your opinion. Councillor Pemberton: It was only on pressure of serving a writ and on the advice of the Town Clerk,who said we should lose- The Town Clerk: Of course, we should. Councillor Pemberton: That we resolved to settle the action. The majority of the Council would not have agreed to paying that sum for it. .It is two years after the committee buys it before the Town Council knows about it. __

The Inspector: You mean you were committed by the action of the committee? Councillor Pemberton : Yes. Mr. Barritt : It took two years before it came before the Council for confirmation. It was only after proceedings had been commenced for specific perform- ance that the Council agreed to the terms. In answer to a ratepayer, the Town Clerk said the Corporation had not purchased the four houses adjoining the works. On the 31st October 1899 the Corporation prescribed a line of frontage. The owner of the works made representations to them that it as his intention to erect other buildings on the land. The Corporation prevented him doing so and then he com- pelled them to purchase the property, because if they had not he could have taken them to arbitration. He (thesTown Clerk) had some knowledge of the valuing of property. He had bought most of it for the Corporation for many years, and Mr. Longdin agreed with him that the price they were paying Mr. Edelsten was a fair and reasonable one and that they would have had to pay more if they had gone to arbitration. Mr. Barritt : Do you mean to argue that on the rateable value of the property, situated as it was then, any valuer would have awarded that figure? The Town Clerk: I say if we had gone to arbitra- tion the probability is we should have had to pay more for it. _

Commenting on the purchase of property at the corner of Scotland.road.,and Horsemarket-street (Mr. Banks' shop, &c.), the Town Clerk said the Corporation made a mistake in only widening Scotland-road to 36 feet. It was apparent that it would need widening again. For the Inspector's information, the Borough Treasurer, now stated that the Corporation's overdraft at the bank amounted to £42,043 17s. including over £ll,OOO owing by property owners for private street works.

HEALTH OF THE DISTRICT.

Dr. SEPHTON reported that during the month there had been 19 births and 16 deaths. The death rate from all causes was equal to 156 per thousand, as against 28 the average of the past three months and 28 the corresponding mouth of last year. The death rate from zymotic diseases (7 principal) was equal to -6 per 1,000 as against 5 the average rate of the last three months and one the corresponding month of last year. Eleven deaths occurred at the County Asylum,Winwick. The death rate proper for the district was equal to 4•9 per thousand of the estimated population. There had also been an outbreak of ihfectious disease in the district. On the proposition of the CHAIRMAN seconded by Mr. HOUGHTON, the report was adopted. The latter alluded to the number of cases of infectious disease, and corn-1 mented on the very low death rate, which he thought

was very remarkable. Dr. SEPFITON said a most remarkable thing was that last month the death.rate was 34, and this month it was only 156, which included the deaths at the Asylum. The death-rate proper for the district last month was 1-P6, and this month it was only 49. Another strange circumstance was that there were no deaths under five !years of age-. _ _

Mr. HOUGHTON: That is a very good report. WINWICK ASYLUM SEWAGE.

The SURVEYOR, reported that he had received a plan from the Winwick Asylum Committee with reference to the taking of the effluent drain from the Asylum Sewage beds along Mill-lane. The plan was approved. FIALY-YEAR'S ESTIMATES.

The CLERK submitted theestimates for the ensuing half-year. For general expenditure the sum of £1.839 would be required, rind a rate of sd. in the .£ would be levied, which was the same as the last half-year Special expenses were as follows :--Burtonwood, £I3O ; Penketh, £80; Rixton, £4O; and Great Sankey £3O. The estimates were approved, on the proposition of Mr. MATHER, seconded by Mr. HOUGHTON.

THE PROPOSED IMPROVEMENT AT SANKEY BRIDGE.

Mr. FURNRSS renorted that he attended the Confer- ence at Preston with regard to the proposed improve- ment at Sankey Canal Bridge. It was an informal Conference with the object of laying before them two alternative schemes, with the idea of getting their sanction to one or the other. One would involve an expenditure of £3,000, and the County Council expected that the London and North-Western Railway Company would bear the expence. The assumption was that they were liable to keep the bridge in repair. Another scheme was laid before them, which it was said would cost £4,000, but if carried out it would cost £6,000. ,Sums were mentioned as to what would have to be paid "by the different authorities with regard to the latter scheme, and the discussion practically concentrated on that. He told the members of the Committee that he could not commit that Council to any expense, whilst they were willing to give them all the svpport possible. The opinion was expressed that the Railway Company were responsible to keep the bridge in condition to meet the current requirements of traffic on the road. The CHAIRMAN: We shall have to wait for develop- ments now.

Mr.-FURNESS : We can go no further until we bear from them.

Mr. M.t.sium proposed a vote of thanks to Mr. Furness, who was appointed along with the Chairman and Surveyor to attend any further meetings in connec- tion with the matter.

Mr. HOUGHTON seconded, and it was agreed to. CROFT WATER SUPPLY.

The SURvEYOR reported that the Angnst gangings of' the Croft wells had been received from the Warrington Corporation Water Engineer. _ _

Mr. HOUGHTON said that item appeared on the minutes month after month and nothing was said about it. He wished to know what was the usefulness of the record. It might be evidence against them some day. The SURVEYOR said the gangings must be taken and sent to this Council sealed and signed. They were simply signed by the Water Engineer in accordance with the Aet. - -

Mr. HOUGHTON: Is there any material variation in the _wells from one year to another ? _ - -

Mr. KERFOOT : SOIRO of them have gone absolutely dry.

-Mr. HOUGHTON remarked that they were not taking any steps to verify those reports. Mr. MATHER said he thought it was done to safe- guard those people whose water was going to be taken by the water works. It was part of the Corporation's duty to deposit the gangings with the Surveyor. Mr. HOUGHTON: We don't know whether it is a correct account or not.

Mr. FURNESS : It is for the individual householders to complain if the water has not been up to their requirements. The CLERK said the report was simply a record. The SURVEYOR said the majority of the wells seemed to be drying up. The discussion then dropped.

OUR LOCAL CHURCHES.

St. James' Church, Latchford

Special sermons were preached at this Church on Sunday on behalf of the Society for-the Propagation of the Gospel in Foreign Parts by the Ron. F. J. Griffiths, of North China. The rev, gentleman gave some inter- esting reminiscences of his experiences in China and elsewhere, and his discourses were closely listened to by exceedingly large audiences. The collections were on behalf of the society, and satisfactory amounts were realized. On Monday, Mr. Griffiths lectured to the school children, and in the evening a large audience assembled in the schoolroom, when the rev, gentleman again interested his hearers with his experiences, and spoke of the importance of the work of the society.

===== 0002601_19070907_art0028.txt | included=true | included =====
New Wesleyan Minister.

Rev. Thomas Miller Preaches at Bold-street. The Rev. Thomas Miller, the newly-appointed minister of the Warrington _circuit, preached his first sermons at Bold-street Wesleyan church on Sunday. There were large congregations. The rev. gentlemisn took for his morning text: "And they that know Thy name will put their trust in Thee; for Thou, Lord, hast not forsaken them that seek Thee" (Psalm ix. 10.) He emphasised the necessity of cultivating a wide outlook in respect to God's government in Nature, human life, and indi- vidual experience. Unbelief was due to a limited out- look and it was a mistake to look only at one side of anything. The last of life for which the first was planned would explain the meaning of problems associ- ated with the earlier stages of national and individual development. We must not be in too great hurry; it was sometimes necessary to suspend judgment. God had plenty of time in which to perfect His plans. The preacher also dwelt upon the need of fear- less study of Nature by Christians. Science• was not an enemy of the faith. The believer was placed under a debt of obligation to the scientist for the new truth discovered. The moral problem varied only in its form of expression. It was the same in essentials. Slums were modern but sin was ancient. The Christian was not dependent on reason only, the revelation of Scripture showed a controlling power. God worked everything for good. The know- ledge of God was most perfectly obtained from the life and works of Christ. The sermon closed with a quotation from Augustine. "Ye must seek as they who find and find as they who have still to seek." In commencing his ministry Mr. Miller urged the need of reconsecratiou to the service of Christ. The Church was a living tree and not a bundle of sticks tied together. Every member gave and received strength through fellowship. There was a splendid congregation at the evening service. The textiwas Ezekiel xvii. 1-3, and the subject "The Continuity of Influence." The influence of carelessness and evil was traced in respect to Israel. The children of those who neglected their duty on account of pleasure, suffered exile. The subject was illustrated by reference to C. A. Dane's letters from Rome. The effect of carelessness in India and • America. Every Christian should take a great interest in politics, the welfare of the town was a Christian question. Individual responsibility was not destroyed by the inheritance of ticious tendencies. Man can, and ought to rise above his environment. What might become by grace, not what we were by nature was of Importance.

Stnet Widening and Electricity Extensions.

MERSEY WORKS PURCHASE DISCUSSED

For six and a half hours, Majpr R. E. Stewart, R.E. one of the inspectors of the Local Government Board, was engaged at the Warrington Town Hall on Tues- day conducting an inquiry into the application of the Corporation for sanction to borrow £40,140 for street widenings and improvements, and £3,071, the sum required in connection with the proposed boiler plant extensions at the electricity works at Howley, and for permission to appropriate for the electricity under- taking, £4,165 being the unexpended balance of a loan raised for similar purposes. Great interest was taken in the proceedings, and there was a large attendance of prominent ratepayers. The Mayor was present, as well as several other members of. the Councils and officials of the Corporation. Mr. Edward L Barritt, solicitor, Manchester, appeared on behalf of the War- rington and District Property Owners' Association and the Warrington Tradesmen's Association. The inquiry began at ten o'clock and, with an hour's interval for luncheon, oontinued until 5.30. The Town Clerk submitted the usual statistical information. The total outstanding loans amounted to £l,l6s,sBs,including £295,306 for public health purposes and £78,718 under local sanitary acts. The average rates for county boroughs amounted to 7s. Bd. in the £ so that Warrington was Bd. below the average. ELECTRICITY EXTENSIONS. The electricity application was first dealt with, the Town Clerk explaining that the loan was required for an extension of the boiler plant. There was a great demand for energy which the engineer was at present put to straits to supply and he could not run the winter through without additional boiler power. In answer to the inspector the Town Clerk said no portion of the loan would go to salaried officials or permanent workmen. The Inspector : It is not considered right to charge some of your permanent workmen to these loans and avold_paying_their wages out of revenue. The Town Clerk: Nothinghas been expended in that way since we received the Board's intimation in February. Mr. P. V. L. Mathias, chief electrical engineer, then gakre evidence. The boiler power was notes great as it ought_to beior the plant._ After this winter they would probably want new generators. • The Inspector asked why they had not the proper number of boilers. Mr. Mathias: Since the last loan of £18,099 we have not put any permanent boilers down. The Town Clerk : It was contemplated at that time to put two new boilers down.We have only put down one. The Inspector: The engineer says: "Oh I have had the money for them but I have not put them down." Why are boilers wanted and not generators? The Town Clerk said Messrs. Preece and Cardew advised the committee in 1903 that they required two engines and two boilers. It was arranged that one boiler and one engine should be purchased eighteen months after the application was made. They anticipated that an additional engine and boiler would be required eighteen months afterwards. Mr. Mathias then came on the scene and thought he could do with one boiler and one engine. They had done without the additional plant for three years and now they found they could not go on any longer. The Inspector: I suppose that since the engineer came he has tried to be its economical is he could. The Town Clark added that the boil& plant had been worked 50 per cent. beyond its capacity. Mr. Mathias then dealt with the great inconvenience caused by the present arrangements for the storage of coal. The Inspector: You have described a terrible state of affairs.- Why have you put up with it so long? Mr. Mathias : It is the old qaestion of capital. The Inspector: You have had to put up with it till you could get your Council to see their way to have the improvement included in a loan? Mr. Mathias : Yes. I thought this would be a suit- able time to have it carried out. The Mayor bore out the engineer's statement and said the condition of affairs had been terrible. In answer to the Inspector, Mr. Mathias said they used cheap coal but they had not been getting proper combustion for some time and therefore had been losing money. lb was stated that sanctions had already been received connection-with the electricity undertaking to the amount of £97,566. Of the last loan of £18,600 the sum still unexpended was £4,165. The principal explanation was that the Council agreed to postpone one engine and boiler. Mr. Barritt said his clients complained of these defer- ments and additional applications which must be expensive. The Inspector said be was informed by the Borough Treasurer that of the loans already sanctioned—£97,so6 —the amount not yet borrowed was £10,500. He asked for that sum to be allocated on the various loans.

Tha matter was investigated in great detail. Mr. Barritt asked what extra expense the town would be put to by deferring the introduction of the second boiler.

Mr. Mathias : I don't think the town Would be put to any further expense. They would save money. Mr. Barritt : When do you anticipate any further money will be required? Mr. Mathias: The generating plant may want ex- tending next winter. The Tokn Clerk: If we had spent the £4,000 on another boiler it would have been lying as dead capital. Councillor Pemberton: Yet when the matter was before the Council it was 'stated that the boilers were urgently wanted.

ttarritt : From the evidence given it appears that the ,estimates Messrs. Preece and Cardew gave were under-estimated.

Mr. Mathias : That I cannot say. So far as I know the estimates were pretty near. Further questioned, Mr. Mathias agreed that if the whole of the work intended to be covered by the £18,600 loan had been carried out, that figure would probably have been exceeded: Mr. Barritt : With regard to to-day's application. Will the money you are applying for cover all the work you intend doing? Mr. Mathias.: It will be as close as I can get it. I have allowed £250 for contingencies. Mr. Barritt :—Do you consider that sufficient on £7,000 ?—I have kept it as low as I could. Judging from past experience don't you think you have allowed too little P—lt would be an easy matter to put the figures higher, but I have kept them down. Would it not pay to buy a better quality of coal, and have better combustion?

111 r. Mathias said that after exhaustive investigations it was found that £1,200 was saved in twelve months by using cheaper coal and mechanical stokers. They might do still better with the cheap coal if they had the best conditions for combustion.

Mr. Barritt : As to the £1,200 saving in coal, did it not rise more from the reduction in the price paid than from anything else? Witness said it was different coal and they generated more units since they used it. The profit had been maintained. _

At this stage the tenders for the boilers, &c. were produced and all were found to be considerably above the estimate. The engineer pointed out that a rise in prices would partly account for this. The Inspector having rrone into the details very exhaustively this part of tie inquiry was closed. STREET lIVIPROVEMENTS.

After luncheon the street widening scheme was con- sidered. The Town Clerk said this part of the applica- tion referred •to £40t140 which the Corporation desired to borrow for street improvements. Under a Provisional Order of 1896 the Corporation got power to prescribe a building line in any street which was narrow or inconvenient. Acting under that power, the Corporation served notices on the Owners of property in all the narrow streets and it was largely owing to those notices having been served that property had been from time to time offered to the Corporation and they had acquired it. Others had been purchased by public auction and others by negotiation when they had come into the ' market. As an indication of the general_policy of he Council the Town Clerk read an extract from Alderman Bennett's " Budget" speech in which he outlined the improvements already accomplished by the Council and stated that the municipal undertakings realized more than those of any other town in England, whereas the rates were considerably below the average for county boroughs.. If the education rate were deducted the rates would only be ss. Bd. Mr. 3ot:7mi remarked that Alderman Bennett's Speech was open to question. The TOWN CLERK said the list of twenty-three properties embodied in the' application had been sent to , the Local Government Board.

The Inspector : What is the whole business going to cost?

• The Town Clerk: We have spent about £250,000 already. Warrington is a very old town—so ancient in

THE OPPOSITION.

Mr. Barritt said the property owners complained very much of the manner in which the properties had been purchased. The purchases dated back as far as 1903 and in some cases considerable time had elapsed before they had come, before the Council. Anyone who had the welfare of his town at heart did not want to give too much away, but in these cases the property owners said the town had been very little considered and pur- chases had been made which the Corporation had no right to make. They complained that the Corporation had no set scheme. These purchases stretched from one end of the town to the other. The Corporation had not persevered in one palticular direction but had acquired properties here and there which seemed to have taken their eye or to have been submitted to them. The Local Government Board ought to condemn the manner of purchase. Councillor Pemberton had been somewhat responsible for the abolishing of the committee which up to a certain time purchased many properties. In other cases the purchases had been left in private hands. The Town Clerk himself had bought I some on instructions by public auction and it had taken a considerable time before the purchases were submitted to the Council for confirmation. No useful purpose ' could be served by the Corporation acquiring some of the properties. His clients 'strongly objected to the Local Government Board giving the Corporation power to borrow money for certain properties. They should be re-sold as they would be of no value to the Corpora- tion at any time and the sooner they were parted with the better. The properties to which particular excep- tion was taken were :—Mr. Hill's property, Silver. Street and Silver-square; Itylands Bros. property at the corner of Church-street and Howley-lane; property,

belonging to the trustees of Jane Naylor in King-street and executors of James Roberts in Hopwood-street; Grace's pottery Winwick-road. In regard to the latter the ostensible object of the purchase was in order that a new school might be built there, but the Education Committee had not made use of the site and had built the Beamont School about half a mile away. Then there was a great discrepancy in the prices given. In some eases the Corporation had made a bargain on behalf of the town but in others the opposition considered the prices exces- sive. In regord to Edelsten's property, his clients thought that in future it ought to be I made impossible for any purchase to bo conducted on those lines. The property owners were ' only too desirous to help the Corporation in any of its well-doing, but at the same time they desired that a firm attitude should be adopted with regard to the ques- tion of dealing with properties, and they thought some representation should be made to the Corporation that they should proceed with some set scheme, and not dodge about from one end of the town to the other. ALDERMAN BENNETT'S REPLY.

Alderman Bennett. as chairmen of the Street Improvement Committee, said he hoped the Inspector would realize the difficulties of the position. Warring- ton was a very old town, and owing to the short- sightedness of their ancestors, it had been developed in ?, very chaotic and higgledy-piggledy manner. It was impossible to go into any part of the town witho,ut seeing some improvement that was almost crying for attention. Unfortunately he was afraid that in some parts of the town, the chaotic process to which he was alluding waa still proceeding. and he hoped the Local Government Board through Parliament would give Cor- porations the power during the next session to develop towns on more reasonable and rational lines. The committee had tried to make the best of the situation. They had primarily devoted their attention to the four main streets which were miserably narrow. While trying to make RS much progress as they could with I those thoroughfares, they had not been indifferent to other opportunities which had occurred. Again and again they had had property brought to their notice in streets which were not suitable to the present needs of the town and they had committed themselves to certain lesser improvements, always, however, arming at the main one which contemplated the four principal streets. There was not a single item on the list of properties which had not been carefully considered by the com- mittee. It was inevitable that there should be a certain amount of secrecy, as when it was known that the Corporation wanted to acquire property there was often an attempt to bleed thern.As to there being any deliberate intention of deceiving the Council or misleading the public,he utterly repudiated that and there was no evidence to justify such a statement. It would be foolish to pretend that every purchase was wise and judicious and in one or two instances the Corporation had made a distinctly bad bargain. He doubted, how- ever, whether it would be wise to re-sell the property and he could hardly contemplate that the opposition desired that the Local Government Board should adopt the other alternative and throw the cost on the rates.

Mr. Barritt : We do not say there have been any tinder-handed dealings. We do not dispute the bona fides of the committee. The Insoector : You said rather that they were foolish. (Laughter.) Mr. Barritt : Yes.

Councillor Pemberton supported the opposition as voiced by Mr. Barritt and said the safeguard given by the Municipal Corporations Act that the Council should be supreme in these matters had been ignored to the extent of thousands of pounds. While he did not question the bona fidcs of the Street Improvement Committee, he objected to them buying property and afterwards prescribing the line, as such a course might involve the town in an improvement to the extent of thousands of pounds which had not been previously ; contemplated by the Council. Mr. S. Gough also objected on similar grounds. He thought the officials had forced the Edelsten purchase upon the Corporation. He believed they led Alderman Bolton into a trap and be signed the agreement and in order to get him out of the trap the Corporation passed' a resolution to indemnify him. They had paid twice as much as the property was worth. Mr. Cliffe also opposed the application and contended that ridiculous prices had been paid in some instances. There was no guarantee as to when the improvements would be carried out. A great (Peal of property which had been already acquired was lying dormant, when it might be well utilized. - - .

Councillor Wilding said he also supported the objection. The Town Clerk said tiara after the temperate way in which Mr. Barritt had put his case, and the able teply of Alderman Bennett, he should only add that the Corporation had bought the property and were anxious to sell the surplus land as soon as possible when the improvements had been carried out. It was quite com- petent for any member of the Council to bring forward a proposal with regard to the disposal of the surplus land. What he asked on behalf of the Corporation was fqr the Board to sanction the loans, and leave it to the Council through the agency of the gentlemen who were objecting that day, to carry out the schemes as soon as possible. The Inspector said he thought they had heard the opposition at length and it was now time to close. The inquiry then terminated. ROPE AND ANCHOR AND THE MERSEY BEDSTEAD WORKS.

At the conclusion of the business of the Town Council meeting, on Tuesday evening, the Town Clerk alluded to the Local Government Board inquiry. He said that at the last meeting of the Council a resolution was passed referring to the Mersey Bedstead Company's Works, and the Rope and Anchor Inn, Buttermarket. street. The Inspector was prepared to include them in the present application if the Corporation would put in the full figures of the purchase money, namely, £4,382 Bs. for the works, and £3,744 12s. for the Rope and Anchor Inn. He wanted the Council to formally do so, and the matter could be ratified at the next Council meeting. _

Councillor Burton asked if the Corporation had to pay the costs of the solicitors. Alderman Forshavv : You don't pay the vendor's costs so far as the Mersey Bedstead Works aro concerned.

The Town Clerk : In the other case we agreed to pay £2O costs for solicitors and surveyors. The Council approved.

WAR.

When Abel fell, through envy slain, Beside his altar low : It was alas, his brother Cain Who dealt the fatal blow. And ever since that awful crime On earth its shadow threw: The Caine of every age and clime Have killed their brothers too. Yet, all nnsatiated still, They wave their banners red : And cry for more recruits to fill The earth with slaughtered dead. Yea, even now our schools are made The sergeant's drilling ground. Where youths may learn the soldier's trade, And step to Bugle's sound. 0 that our working men were wise, In all these things to scan The hand of Satan in disguise Against poor erring man. True to himself that Demon still Would blind us with a lie; Still lure us on to do his w.ill, And in his service die. ' But why should Christians bear the sword And fellow creatures slay ? Are these things sanctioned by their Lord, Fruits of " the living way ? Ah no, His Gospel's from above, An antidote for strife ; A grace that binds His flock in love, And leads to endless life. September 1907. J. H. W. CLINDINNING.

THE President of the Trades

OLD-AGE Union Congress talks as if PENSIONS, AND in national affairs it were THE COST. not necessary to take finan-

cial considerations into account,inasmuch as a Chancellor of the Exchequer with a majority behind him can raise as much money as he likes by increasing taxation and by borrowing freely. The study of questions of national finance from this point of view has, it must be admitted, been greatly encouraged by South African War experiences. We then increased annual expenditure by more than double the cost of the realisation of the most expensive old-age pension scheme that has yet been thought of, whilst also adding immensely to the National Debt. But the consequences have, surely,not been a matter of congratulation,as seems to be assumed by those who think, or appear to think, that the more a nation is burdened by taxation the happier it must be? We shall be paying heavily for years to come fct the extrava- gances of the South African War ; and, whatever Mr. GILL may think, the paymeLt will have to come out of the pockets of the producers. It has not yet ceased to be true that, as JOHN BRIGHT stated the case, a stone set . rclling down stairs bounds from step to step until it lies, heavy and inert, at the bottom. Mr. GILL ',declares that the only satisfactory solution of the old-age pension question would be a scheme that would be universal, non-contributory, and non-discriminatory. This means that when they arrived at a certain age all the members of the community would be entitled to draw an old- age pension, the MARQUIS OF CARABAS equally with BILL JAKES. The President of the Trades Union Congress proposes to put the age at sixty. Are we all too old at sixty? Few of us would be willing to admit that, unless it were for the purpose of obtaining a pension. When a man took his pension it is to be pre- sumed that he would retire from work; but why should he do so if he is in good health and is earning good wages? This is one aspect of the case that is worthy of some consideration. There are no calculations to show what a ss. a week pension commencing at sixty would cost to the nation. The President of the Trades Union Con- gress has, in fact, flown a little higher than other , theoriaecs on the subject. It would cost, at any rate, ever £30,000,000 a year. Where is that very ' large amount to come from? Mr. GILL declines to %rimer the question, and even to consider it. He thrown the responsibility on the CHANCELLOR of the EXCHEQUER. "It is DO part of our duty to show where the money can be found," he ' remarks. That is as it may be.

"Aim high" is a classic and mc.st admirable injunction. It is Americanised and illuminated by EMERSON in the famous phrase, " Hitch your waggon to a star." Mr. GILL certainly aims high enough, for he aims at something out of sight. We must admit, however, that there is provocation for doing this in such proposals as that which SIR ALFRED LYALL, SIR EDWARD 131/AIIIIOOK, and LORD AVEBURY have addressed to the " Times." Admitting that old-age pensions are inevitable, these titled persons set out a scheme for reducing them to a minimum, that ia, for pay- ing out as little as possible. Nobody would be entitled to benefit from the State except on his own contributions, and the extent of the benefit would be, say, an extra 1 per cent. on the Savings Bank rate. Here is a maximum, with a vengeance. Between the maximum of Mr. GILL and the minimum of Lord AVEBURY and his friends the CHANCELLOR of the EXCHEQUER has "room and verge enough" for really every variety of practi- cable scheme. And, of course, any scheme pro- posed to Parliament by the Government must be of a quite practicable and reasonable character. It must not be of the wild-cat variety.

We hope our readers BOROUGH FINANCE. will study the lengthy extracts from the Borough Treasurer's annual financial statement which we print in another column. There is a mass of information given which is well worth the thought- ful consideration of every ratepayer who takes an interest in municipal government and cares to know what it costs. There are no very striking features in the report, which contains the usual aggregate balance statement showing a balance of borough assets over liabilities of £456,542 us. The returns from various departments are of chief interest. For instance the accounts of the Town Hall, Parks and Baths Committee show that there has been, during the year, an expenditure of ..-C25 on the bowling green at Victoria Park ; caretaker's wages have amounted to £5B 18s., and the recdipts from bowlers total ; £54 10s. The green is obviously filling a very use- ful and commendable purpose at an infinitesimal annual cost. We hope the experiment, having now proved its success, will be proceeded with in other parts of the town. Bowling greens are of equal value to the town's people as public baths, and we notice that the latter have" made "a loss of £256 16s. 6d.—which is not a great deal. The town might well spend £l,OOO a year on the pro- vision of recreative and bathing facilities for the public.

Warrington is still far BEAUTIFUL from beautiful—Alderman WARRINGTON. Bennett so recently as Saturday described it as "intolerably ugly" in many places—but the members of the Beautiful Warrington Society are working hard to improve it. Their efforts have been attended with a considerable measure of success, as witness the brighter appearance of the streets through the more liberal introduction of flowers. At one time the idea of holding a suc- cessful horticultural show in Warrington would have aroused pitying scorn, but Saturday saw the second exhibition of the kind which the Society has promoted and no-one could deny that it was a great., credit to al grimy manufacturing town. It can no longer be ; questioned that choice blooms will grow in War- rington, and there is every encouragement to the Society to persevere in its somewhat ambitious programme. The most gratifying feature of all is that the juvenile section is making splendid progress, no fewer than 1300 plants cultivated by the children being submitted to the judges. AL praise is due to the teachers, and particularly to Mr. F. H. Longshaw, the secretary of the section, who are doing their utmost to imbue the youngsters with a love for the beautiful. As the Mayer pointed out, however, much remains to be done in this direction, for gardens still suffar through wanton depredations. Nothing can be more discouraging to the house- holder who carefully tends his little piece of land than to have pretty flowers pulled up by the roots, and until the rising generation is sufficienbly impressed with the evil of such vandalism Warrington will not become the beautiful place that we all desire it to be.

The discussions at the THE VALUE CF THE Trades Union Congress TRADES-UNION prove that this, the oldest CONGRESS. of the federations of trade

organisations in our country, has not lost its ancient claim to be regarded as the real "Parliament of Labour." Where else can the Marxian, the Liberal-Labourist, and the CJonservative-Labourist, with all their intermediate grades, meet together in a common conclave? The basis of the Labour Party excludes from its councils all who do not pledge themselves to political action apart from the two ancient parties in the State; the doings at the annual meeting of the General Federation of Trades Unions are never heard of by the man in the street. It is true these two organisations have machinery for carrying into effect: the resolutions passed, and 'can, therefore, afford to stigmatise the Trades Union Congress as "a mere talking shop. Perhaps however, this may be regarded as only another 1 way of stating that it is,, a Parliament, and its discussions, if they tend to be informal, are not , therefore useleis. It may be true that the secession of the Engineers marks the beginning of a move- ment which will eventually reduce the Congress to a position of much less importance, but it must be admitted that hitherto it has filled a place which no other body attempts to fill, and its mediation is likely to bring concord into the divided ranks of Labour if anything will. We note that a usually highly-respectable London paper has stated that some of the delegates "were distinctly seen to Ishudder on being told they would have to go to Bath." The suggestionis ungentlemanly,especially as the journal would be one of the first to claim superior manners for the classes whose views it voices.

The King's Speech had a , INDIA'S "PAUSING reference to India which DIFFICULTIES." may stir up the melancholy critics. We shall hear again ' that people in this country do not think sufficiently of India—nor do they, verily. It is inconceivable to most folks that the British Empire should be boxed up like a hat and sent packing. Yet what is there but prestige against it? Out of every 10,000 persons in India, bay the Jeremiahs, thirty- four males and three and a half females only are returned as "literate in English," and there are but 2,000 British officers in a native Army of 140,000. The entire British garrison in India is 74,000. Is it not conceivable that that may be adequate for all the total population of 294 millions? But there is another menace, say the critics. We have heard much of it lately. The day is coming, and now is, when the Bengali by examination papers threatens to oust the entire Imperial service. Between these two stools we fall. That is the judgment of the lugubrious critic whose imagina- tion is affected by what happened fifty years ago. Dr. W: H. Fitchete, who was in India last year, has written a series of articles in the Tribune to show both sides of the case, and he is the more entitled to speak because he has just seen with his own eyes modern problems and historic scenes, and has already manufactured spirited books upon this theme. The pessimists must not be allowed to have it all their own way. India knows what England has done for her, and she knows, more- over, to-day in what her welfare consists.

W. HODGKINSON, LINITTEP:

NOTES AND NOTIONS.

Readers who are admirers of the Park Aviary and Aquarium will be interested to learn that it costs £32 12s. 9d. to feed the birds and fishes. * * * ' There have been three picnics from St. Clement's Mission during the month—one to Blackpool, another to Hollinfare, and a third to Bank-street. So the humorous writer in the Parish Magazine informs us. It transpires that the Band of Hope had arranged a picnic to Preston Brook, but the rain descended so copiously that they never left Bank-street, the outing being postponed until a more favourable day. • 0 One way to shorten the Council meetings is to tire out some of the verbose members earlier in the day. The Local Government Board Inquiry on Tuesday satiated the mayor and others so much that His Worship appealed to members to cut their remarks short at the Council meeting. For a wonder they did so, and the business was got through without any argument worth speaking of in about 20 minutes. * _

Who shall take upon himself to say, " I adjudge Lord Kelvin's work to be worth so many hundred brass State tokens, and that of John Beetle, the paviour, to be worth a dozen, and, Amen, thus shall they be paid?" This also savours not of sweet content in your pattern Commonwealth.—" Socialist," in the Standard."

A Kingston tradesman, having stocked his window with a hair restorer guaranteed to produce" a luxuriant growth" on almost anything, a fellow tradesman and friend sent him a worn-out broom to be treated in appreciation of the advertisement. Not to be outdone, the hairdresser purchased a new broomhead and returned it to his facetious friend, together with a bill for the cost of the new crop! The account was promptly met by the would-be joker.

_ COG- Ice in September is the latest phase of the climatic vagaries. Two degrees of frost were registered during Tuesday night at Eccleston, near Chester, and on Wednesday morning there was a thin coating of ice on the small. pools in the neighbourhood. It will be remembered that about this time last Year we were enjoying a temperature approaching 90 degrees. Needless to say, if the frost continues great havoc will be wrought amongst the crops. * * * The Limerick " craze " this week was strongly in evidence at Padgate, for at the Garden Fête held in connection with the Padgate Parish Church three prizes were offered for the best last line. The verse is about a young lady named Kate and the Garden Fete. Whatever may be the fate of the young lady named Kate at the Garden Fete there can be no question as to the ultimate fate of the proceeds. They will be swallowed up in good works—at least le , us hope so.

[Green bowler hats for men are to be fashionable this autumn.] Oh, Paddy, dear, and did you hear the news that's going round? Green bowlers are the only wear where Irishmen are found, And when the verdant billycock adorns each Irish head, We'll show the hated Sassenach the Green above the Red!

"London Opinion." a o There was not much light thrown on the Sankey Canal Bridge improvement scheme at the meeting of the Town Council on Tuesday. Apparently, the new bridge will cost the London and North-Western Rail- way Company £3,000 or £4,000, but there is no liability on the part of any local authority like War- rington to find any of the money. The suggestion from the county authorities that Warrington should find -5750 seems to have been uncalled for, and it has certainly been firmly repudiated. Anyhow there is a prospect of the scheme being taken in hand before very long. No one will be sorry to see the last of the paltry wooden structure which has to take all the road traffic between Warrington and Liverpool. * * * The" Glasgow Evening News " commenting on the Duke of Argyll getting 241 years' purchase for the land at Kilcreggan says the case is on a level with long prices in connection with Rosyth and other places, and further slggests "that our great land- owners should set a good example instead of seeking to screw the last penny out of the nation and extort prices they would get from no private buyer." No fantastic-will-o'-the-wisp-hypothetical value here —it is the plain feu-value, so much scoffed at by the tub-thumping opponents of the Land Values Bill in Parliament. Nor could the Professors of Political Economy make out how such land could be valued for building purposes. It does not seem to have given the Duke's agents much trouble. These gentlemen should have been up before the Select Committee. 0 0

In succession to Mr. Lloyd George, the Right Hon. Sidney Buxton, M.P. (Postmaster-General), who has early in the year addressed a Liberal demonstration at Warrington, accepted the presidency of the Young British Liberals Federation, and will deliier his presidential address on Saturday, October 12th. This year Manchester has been chosen as the place of meeting. In the morning a session of the National Congress will be held when the question of amalgama- tion with the League of Young L;berals will be finally settled. A public conference will occupy the afternoon and resoluti-ons concerning Government appointments, the House of Lords, a Ministry of Commerce, &c., will be debated. In the evening a reception will be given and Mr. Buxton will make an important speech upon the work of the recent Parliamentary session. The signal success of last year's meetings. when Mr. Lloyd George made his historic re-entry into Birmingham, is likely to be repeated.

* The Local Government Board inquiry held at the Town Hall on Tuesday was one of the most protracted affairs of its kind that we have had for some time. The amount involved was large—some S.43,ooo—and the inspector investigated the why and wherefore of the expenditure in great detail. As was to be expected, the economists had their say, the Property Owners' and Tradesmen's Associations being legally represented, and criticisms were freely passed. 'The greater part of the money is wanted for Istreet widening purposes, and occasion was taken to protest against the Corporation being com- mitted to the purchase of property without the sanction of the Council. The discussions on this vexed question will be fresh in the minds of our readers, and it is only necessary to remark that they culminated in the abolition of the Property Purchasing Committee, so that the Council is now in a position,to exercise a tighter hand in these important matters. Even Alderman Bennett could not justify all the pur- chases made being judicious and it is imperative that strict regard should be paid to the interests of the ratepayers in expending these large sums.

It is seldom that the musical world sustains within a few days two such losses as the death of Joachim and Grieg—the foremost violinist and one of the most distinguished composers of the day. The personality of Joachim (says the London correspondent of the " Liverpool Post and Mercury ") was familiar to the London concert-goer. Grieg was known mainly through his writings. His visits to England were infrequent, but when he came among us last summer, and conducted an orchestral concert at Queen's Hall, the enthusiasm of his reception proved abundantly the depth of the affection in which he was held in this country. Grieg takes high rank among modern composers. His fascinating gifts cannot justly be compared with the mighty genius of Wagner or Tschaikowsky, but as a poet of nature, an interpreter of the spirit of his own land, its mountains, fjords,and forests, and the folk-lore of its people, and their trans- lator into music of the daintiest and most piquant character, be has no equal. His works have long been among the first favourites in London concert halls. The "Peer Gynt "Suite No. 1 never fails to captivate, and during the promenade season of the Queen's Hall scarcely a week passes without it figur- ing in the programme. Indeed, during the present season it is down for performance twice in more than one week.

The canine "confidence trick "" was played on me most persistently the other morning (writes a " Man- chester Guardian " correspondent). I have a walk of about two miles to the railway station, and soon after leaving home I noticed an Irish terrier plodding along some twenty yards behind me. To test the animal's intentions I stopped and looked over a hedge. He stopped also, and affected to be totally unconscious of my existence. I walked on; se did he. I crossed the road ; he followed, still keeping the same distance between us, and carefully looking anywhere but in my direction. "Go home! ' I shouted to him sternly, whereupon he looked up with an air of troubled simplicity, as if to say, "It's not your road, is it ? " There was no time to argue, so I walked on quickly, and he continued to trot after me. He had, in fact adopted me, and it was impossible to lose him. Close by the station, however, he turned into an entry, and I fairly ran down the steps and on to the platform, to the astonishment of two or three friends. The train came in a moment after, I got into a carriage and slammed the door. As the train began to move, that precious dog came down the steps and hurried along the platform, glancing earnestly along the line of carriages. I felt as mean as if Inhad deceived him and left him in the lurch.

* * The report that coal is to go up five shillings a ton before the winter, reminds a Manchester Guardian" correspondent of a story he was told some years ago by one of the oldest inhabitants of Upholland, near Wigan. One winter, early in the nineteenth century, coal was unusually dear and poor folk hard put to it to keep themselves and their children from freezing to death. This tragic winter brought a new terror to the village. It was whispered that a ghostly funeral procession could be seen almost any night passing through the churchyard. At Upholland there is a public path which bisects the churchyard, and it was along this path that the funeral passed. Many people coming home late at night saw it, and their descriptions tallied—a white coffin with bearers and mourners all clothed from head to foot in white. At last some llold spirits agreed to watch, and on the very first night they were rewarded by seeing the ghostly procession slowly walking through the churchyard. Instead of running away at this proof of the genuineness of the apparation, the watchers approached in a body. Thereupon the ghostly pro- cessionists dropped the coffin and took to their heels. The coffin was found to be full of coal, and further inquiries discovered that wholesale stealing of coal had been carried on for some time by a gang of men. Coal not being an easy thing to steal on a large scale, they had hit on this method of safe transport.

EARLESTOWN AND NEWTON,

HUMANITY 'FIRST.

At a Labour demonstration at Bristol on Sunday, Mr J. A. Seddon, M.P., spoke of the necessity of old-age pensions, and asked bow long would they treat aged poor worse than the law permitted them to treat animals. Humanity stood first with the Labour party whether towards children or aged poor. LORD NEWTON DEFENDS THE LORDS. CHECK AGAINST "RECKLESSNESS."

I"The threats and denuniciations levelled against the 1 House of Lords by a Socialistic and Radical Govern- ! ment " were dealt-with by Lord Newton and other 1 speakers at a Primrose League demonstration held at Garswood Hall on Saturday afternoon.—Lady Gerard, Dame President of the Newton-le-Willows Branch of the Primrose League,. presided, and explained the ; principal objects of their organisation. The maintenance 'of the Constitution, she said, was a vital principle of , the Primrose League, and they must be firm and united ' in this now that it was being attacked not only by the , 1 Socialists but also by a Radical Government. They , , had -two members of the House of Lords with them that 1 , afternoon, and both of them were on their trial. The : Radicals told them that the Hquse of Lords were I opposed to reform' and yot Mr. Winston Churchill was ; busily advertising the fact that . the Government had : passed forty measures in twenty-one days.—(latighter)— , Mr. Makse, editor of the" National Review," moved a resolution of want of confidence in the present Govern . !ment, and of "emphatic protest against the revolu- tionary policy of the present Radical and Socialistic ! Government.' —Lord Newton said it was not within the ' bounds of human probability that he would ever stand ' again for the Newton division, because, in spite of what I was said against the House of Lords, he did not think , that House would come to an end in his lifetime. The i' old Conservative spirit in that division was as strong as ever, and they were only waiting for an opportunity to prove the strength of their position. (Cheers.) He , did not suppose there was anybody present who had any confidence in the present Government. He sometimes 1 I wondered if their own supporters had that confidence in • their leaders which they professed. The Government . was known to cherish strong Socialistic proclivities, but it was interesting to observe that the Socialists appeared to be as adverse to toe Government as the Conservatives were. (Laughter.) They had no con- fidence in the Government and the same Could be said of the Home Rulers. Lord Gerard and himself appeared before them more or less in the character of political criminals. According (to the Liberal opposi- tion they could not do right. If a measure dealing with the working classes was sent up to the Lords they were told that they should have no voice in the matter, because they could not understand anything about the working classes, and that they had no business to have any opinion, much less express one, on a subject of that kind. If, on the other hand, a measure was sent up dealing with the land—a question upon which they were supposed to have some knowledge—they were told that it was grossly unfair for them to deal with it or vote upon it because they were interested and prejudiced persons. A vast mass of undigested measures were rushed through the House of Commons by the weight of a purely mechanical majority without giving the apposition time to discuss them. These measures were afterwards sent to the House of Lords with the intimation that they were to be pissed in the course of a few days—if possible in the course of a few hours—without material alteration. This kind of procedure was supposed to represent the will of the people—the will those who belonged to Radical and Socialist clubs, and of those who voted for Radical candidates: Whatever defects there were in the House of Lords, and he admitted that a diligent scrutiny may discover some defects, it was still an effective check against anti-national and reck- less legislation. (Cheers.) He seconded the resolution. —Colonel R. Pilkington maintained that the Govern- ment introduced measures out of mere spite. Their opponents were doing their best to stir up a campaign against the House of Lords. He believed that if some of the measures had been passed without revision by the House of Lords they would have wrecked the cquntry.—The resolution was passed, and the meeting ' adcorded a vote of thanks to Lady Gerard for presiding.

The fortnightly meeting of this Board was held on Tuesday at Dutton Workhouse. There were present : Mr. Thomas Baxter Oa the chair) Messrs. J. Spruce, T. Wright. W. M. Morris, S. Woodward, C. E. Linaker senior, Peter Booth, T. Collins, A.Haining, C. Roylance, J. Myneky me, J. Douglas, J. Hallows, J. Y. Davies, G. Pilling, J. W. Temperley, J. L. Jones, G. Egerton- Warburton, A. H. Talbot and J. Stokes, G. F. Ashton (clerk), J. Cook, W. R. dePrez, and J. Pearson (relieving officers). TETE VAGRANCY QUESTION.

The Bodmin Union asked the Guardians to pass a resolu- tion impressing upon the Local Government Board and His Majesty's Government the necessity of at once dealing with the question of vagrancy:The difficulty aris- ing through the alarming increase in the number of tramps, the impracticability of suitably employing them in the workhouses at a profit and,they reared, the serious menace in the future to the peace and well-being of the country, led the Board to the conclusion that the most practical solution of the difficult problems was the establishment of labour colonies of the Government works at which vagrants should be compulsory employed on remunerative labour. They believed that such employment under proper regulations might be not only of great importance to the vagrants themselves but also of financial benefit to the State. Consideration of the letter was deferred on the motion of Mr. Morris, until the Board received the report of the deputation to the North Western Poor Law Coaference,,at which the Clerk said this subject would be dealt with.

The Committee had arrived at their decision almost unani- mously, but they had not the acceptance by the medical officers, and until this was settled it would be better that the matter should remain where it was. Lae joined the force in 1883, and commenced duties at Wigan, afterwards at Prescot and Rochdale. In 1894 he' was promoted to the rank of sergeant. MEASLES EPIDEMIC AT RUNCORN. The monthly meeting of the Runcorn Urban District Council was held on Wednesday, Mr. Thomas Jones presiding. The Medical officer reported that during August there were 24 deaths and 50 births, as compared with 28 deaths and 48 births in the corresponding month of last year. The Local Government Board wrote pointing out that in the quarter ending June 30th there were 28 deaths from measles, and they asked for a report from the medical officer as to the origin of the disease, and the measures taken to prevent the spread of the disease. The Council instructed Mr. td`Dougall to furnish the reporc without delay. BRAVE RUNCORN BOY. In the presence of the assembled scholars of Runcorn Parish Church School, on Wednesday, the Royal Humane Society's certificate on vellum, in a handsome gilt frame, was presented to Master Harry Rogers, son of Mr. Arthur Rogers, butcher, of Egerton-street, Runcorn, for his bravery in saving his younger brother from. drowning in the Ship Canal. Councillor Littler, J.P., made the presentation, and extolled the boy's gallant conduct and exhorted his fellow-scholars to emulate his manliness and courage. The teachers of the Parish Church Sunday School have also presented Rogers with a valuable silver watch and chain. -

- - - RUNCORN INSTITUTE SECONDARY SCHOOL. At the meeting of Runcorn Urban District Council on Wednesday, Mr. T. Jones presiding, the Board of Edu- cation forwarded a copy of an application made by the Cheshire County Council Education Committee for a new scheme for the management of the Runcorn Insti- tute Secondary School. The scheme included a govern- ing body of thirteen members (of whom three were to be women), seven appointed by the County Council, three by the Urban Council, two by the Rural Council, and one by Liverpool University. The Board of Edu- cation pointed out that the trust deed of the institute gave the council no power of letting the promises as a secondary school, and their use for such a school was irregular and unauthorised. The board, therefore, invited the council to apply for authority to use the premises for the purposes for education other than those provided by the deed. Mr. Marshall (secre- tary) narrated the circumstances which led to the institute being used as a secondary School, and said although it looked on the face of it as though the council had done an illegal thing, they had the sanction of the Science and Art Department for what had been libne A new room, to which Sir John Brunner gave g3OO, was built at a cost of £l,OOO to meet the require- ments of that departatent.—The subject was referred to a committee for full consideration.

For some time Mrs. Frodsham managed to support herself and the four children, but ultimately the guardians had to pay Gs. per week for the other children. WHITE CITY, MANCHESTER.

With a best of attractions covering almost every field of entertainment, the White City is a feature no one can afford to ignore. At no place in Europe has the field of amusement been more thoroughly covered. It gives the place a distinctive individuality and is in a class entirely by itself. It has met with public favour since the opening and enjoys a most profitable season. For the lovers of good music and genuine, open-air diversion it is enjoying the full benefit of an ever increasing popularity. The Best Bands of Great Britain furnish the best concert music ever beard in Manchester every afternoon and evening, and additional interest lends itself to these Concerts by the introduction of well-known soloists. The people of Lancashire have patronised this one ,-air Amusement Resort because it was what they wanted, and while the weather has been all but favourable, crowds have been attracted here by the beauty of its surroundings, diversity of amusement, its many 'Free Attractions, as well as the courteous treatment bestowed on them. The electrical display at night is the finest ever seen, it is a sight worth travelling miles to see. Every place in the Park where electricity mild be utilised for illuminating and decorating purposes it has been. The concessions are everything they should he,for the Managing Director John Calvin Brown, has taken every precaution to protect the public in such a manner that neither life, limb or purse could be injured. The annual Dahlia and ,ICottagers Show of the Royal Horticultural Society of I Manchester vt ill be held in the White City on Tuesday and Wednesday next, September 10th and 11th. In addition to the classes for dahlias and vegetables there will be a fine display of herbaceous flowers, phloxes, sweet peas, etc., making in all a highly attractive exhibition. The show will opfm at 12.30 each day. Another special feature, to be introduced on Friday. September 20th. is the engagement of the celebrated Gipsy violinist. Josef Ronsev, who will give his first concert in England at the White City: Rousey has just arrived from Marienbad where he was comnanded to play before H.M. the King.- Details later.
