CHRIST AND SOCIALISM. In a former letter I replied to Mr. Charles E. Parker's criticism of my interpretation of the parable of the labourers in the vineyard, and to his question Whether I would tell him how Socialism harmonises with the teachings of Christ. Mr. Parker now raises some Ifresh points and asks farther questions to wbich I will endeavour to reply. Before doing so, however, I would like to say how much I disliked the concluding sentence of his letter. It ran: If I have occasion again to write on this subject I will deal with the somewhat foolish and 'short-lived attempt of the early Church to establish Socialism." Such a remark from so amiable a gentle- man was altogether unexpected, for the only inference that one can draw from it is that because the early Church _failed to establish ,Socialism therefore ! Christianity and Socialism are incompatible. It may be of course an ebullition of splenetic satire, or it may show a deficiency of argument. To a man of ordinary reasoning abilities,it is a mere jargon of words ! strung together for an object not altogether happy. We know already why the Communist experiment at Jerusalem failed. We know why the churches to-day are failing, too. We shall, however, be pleased to hear Mr. Parker's version of it later. For the moment I think it would be better to continue the original line of argument. Mr. Parker, in his last letter, said that I persistently ignored what to him seemed the central thought of the parable of the labourers in the vineyard, viz : The householder's question : "Is it not lawful for me to do what I will with mine outn ?" That question, he

says, is a clear declaration of individual rights. lam sorry if I ignored it. It was not intentional. I was always under the impression that a question was not a declaration. Like Pilate' s question, —" what is truth ? "—this too was left unanswered. If we are anxious to know what is the "law " we can find it. All the law is fulfilled. in one word, even in this : ".Thou shalt love they neighbour as thyself." Again : "Bear ye one another's burdens, and so fulfil the law of Christ." And again : "We then that are strong ought to bear the infirmities of the weak, and not to please ourselves." It seems to me, therefore, that if the householder was carrying out those injunctions he was quite at liberty to do what he " willed " with his own. If these isolated texts or parables are to be deprecated, for a clear exposition one can read the fifth chapter of St. Matthew and the Ist Corinthians, twelfth chapter. Mr. Parker claims thtit the whole of Christ's teach- ing aims at regeneration from within as opposed to the Socialist's regeneration from without. That appears to me like begging the question. For, if -we remssnber reading how the regeneration of the disciples began, we shall know that Christ sNid : "Follow Me,and I will make you fishers of men." And we are told they straightway left their nets, forsook all their personal belongings, and followed Him. Regeneration, no matter whether it is from within or without, can only come in one way —the sacrifice of "self." And, besides, what does it matter how it begins as long as it do begin. What was the first miracle of Christ? Was it from ! Within or without? What happened at Ills first passover ? And so I 'night go on. Christ's teaching aimed at regeneration, either from within or without, according to circumstances, by removing the obstacles which prevented a man from living that fuller life He came to teach. What we want to know is whether the present competitive system produces pernicious external habits? For external habits affect the mode of life, and the mode of life makes or mars the internal, as well as the external character of the man. Is it of any use a man or woman attempting to follow Christ if he or she has to live by cheating another? Each charge towards an unselfish co-operative system will establish a substantial and pernaanen't good, unattended by counteracting evils. Mr. Parker says: Socialism seeks to compel men to do right by the intervention of the State, Christ seeks to make men rejoice by regenerating the heart and life. Socialism compels Christ makes. There are here two forms of compulsion one from without, the other from within. Let him choose which horn of the dilemma he likes. If men do wrong the State will punish them. If they sin against God hell awaits them. Is it not better, therefore, for the State to make it easy for men to do right and hard for them to do wrong? And cannot that be done by removing the stumbling blocks? Mr. Parker speaks of regenerating the " heart." How can the " heart " be regenerated whilst the body is being pulled out of shape? Mr. Parker misrepresents Socialism in the parable of the selfish brother. Socialists do not want to make all men equal in possessions. What they do want is that the country, and all the machinery of pro iuction in the country, shall belong to the whole people to be used by the people and for the people. Mr. Parker emphasizes the notion that the Kingdom of Heaven is something only in the next world, and that men are set only to save what liingsley called "their own dirty souls" Frederick Denison Maurice, whom Charles Kingsley delighted.to call " Master " said: I seriously believe that Christianity is the only foundation of Socialism, and that a true Socialism is the necessary result of a sound Chti.4ianity. _ _

.Ifany of the mi;representations of Socialism proceed from insufficient knowledge. Socialists are not mere theorists, visionariPs, dreatnsrs. They wish to link on to their politics humanity —ideals, if you like. They want men and women and children to be happy. If anyone, can show them a better way than the one they have chosen it is their duty to do so. But, please, do not ask. us to be so uncharitable as to insist upon every man aetting the fruit of his labor, the whole fruit., and nothing but the fruit. It is impracticable and un-Christian.

JOHN WORKM.IN PURE AND UN ADULTERED SOCIA.LISU.

I hasten to express my deep sympathy with your contributor "Socialist Trades Unionist," as finding he holds an entirely illogical position, he is necessarily forced to adopt petty, abusive and spiteful tactics. This ardent enthusiast „actually asserts "he would not attempt to discuss contentionkpoints of policy and administration in the Voice of Labour," whilst he has .already written a couple of columns 'advocating a "political alliance" being arranged with the local Socialist and Trades Union branche4. Does he really not know that there are many thousands of British Socialists (no less earnest than himself) who are bitterly opposed to any compact of this description, and is he not aware that this subject is undoubtedly one of the most contentious points of policy at present refeiving the consideration of the Britcsii Socialist movement? Then in his own little-naindod style he amusingly informs your readers that "Commonsense "is either un-attached ' or a ' party-wrecker.' " Does Socialist Trade-Unionist sneer in this silly fashion at alt com- rades who differ fr.lrn him on any points of policy and also sarcastically describe as "no-attached" every Socialist, who is not an " I.L.P.er " say for example such men as H. M. Hyudman, Robert .Blatchford and G. Bernard Shaw? _

"Socialist Trades Unionist" represents a type of individual who is gradually becoming less influential in the councils of our Socialist movement and is being superseded by a more intelligent and less ungentle-' manly class of advocate who does not invariably insult his opponents and ridicule his colleagues who venture to express different opinions to himself on details of administration and policy. In reply to your correspondent I wish to say that I believe that both Trades Unionists and non-unionists would materially advance .their best interests by vigorously and continuously supporting the cause of Socialism, and in the words of Jean Jaures, the talented leader of the French Socialists, I unhesitat- ingly declare that" The domination of one class is an attempt to degrade humanity. Socialism, which will abolish all primacy of class, and indeed all class, restores humanity to its highest level. It is therefore a duty for all men to be Socialists." COMMON-SENSE. CAPITAL AND LABOUR

It is said (and believed) sometimes that the interests of capital and labour are identical. This supposed identity of interests is assumed to be proved by the discovery that unless capital pays high wages it cannot command efficient labour. 'There is no identity of interest between the wage payer and the wage earner.

===== 0002601_19070907_art0072.txt | included=false | skipped/gap: fewer than 20 non-control characters after cleanup =====
Ea

===== 0002601_19070907_art0073.txt | included=true | included =====
.11 The efficient labour which high wages produce is st ir bought and sold by capital, is still employed o rejected as it snits the convenience of capital, is still treated as possessing no rights of its own but as ministering to the interests of others. This opposi- tion may be and often is termed the class war, and I would submit to members of the Social Democratic Federation that the Trade Unionism (old type) they often revile is in reality the purest expression of this simple antagonism between capital and labour. Trades Unionism is based on the assumption, borne out by experience, that capital will do its utmost to exploit labour, and that labour ought to do its best to prevent capital succeeding. It is a plain recognition of an obvious industrial fact. The interest of wage earning is the sole concern of the old type of Trade Unionist, and its horizon is bounded by the factory and hi i workshop. The broad-minded Social Reformer therefore, by what- ever label distinguished, is glad to see that the new development of trade union effort is less of a class war character' but seeks to better the conditions of life generally by use of pilitical machinery and educa- tional propaganda, in order that methods of taxation and communal use and control of wealth may be exercised so as to reduce the unequal burden of the workers. It is a natural law that the organism dies which does not adjust itself to changing conditions and environment and is law applies to trades unions and other bodies whiPh are themselves part of the social organism. Let us study more the laws of life and apply them more fearlessly to the social body Let us remember that "New occasions teach new duties Time makes ancient good uncouth, He must ever march right onward Who would keep abreast of truth." _

LAZARUS. NOTICE TO CORRESPONDENTS.

Many thanks to Mr. H. Whitham for sending me a copy of "The Pinkerton Labour Spy." it certainly sheds light on the inky darkness and secrecy so neces- sary to the successtof detective agencies. It is passing strange to find men in this 20th century who will so forget themselves as to belie their fellow-men. Warrington _Draper's Failure

At the Warringt,e Bankruptcy Court, yesterday, b.fore Mr. Registrar Ridgway, Aaron Clarke residing at 239, Liven)9ol-road, Warriagtou, and carrying on business as a draper at a stall in the. Market Hall, apt-eared: for his fpublic examination in bankruptcy. Bankrupt admitted th it in Maya, 1900, when he started business on his own account he was insolvent to the extent of £lB9, and that his deficiency had been increased to its present amount of £330 2s. 2d. by loss on trading owtog to heavy travelling expenses to various markets. He stated that he commenced busi- ness as a draper, in partnership with Mr. Thomas Spencer, in Augu-t, 1905, at a stall in the Market Hall, without capital, but the concern was really .carried on by them on behalf of his former employer who recei,red a percentage of the profits instead of the lamp stun of £175 winch debtor mentioned in his statement of affairs. The partnership was determined in March, 1906, each partner taking over half of the liabilities and assets of the business. The liabilities taken over by bankrupt including £125 half td the bank overdraft, amounted to £242; and the assets consisted of stock valued at cost prices at £53. The bankrupt further stated that, in March, 1906, he re-commenced a similar business on his own account at another stall in the Market, with the liabilities and assets referred togibove ; that he carried it on uotil 15th July last, when he instructed an auctioneer to sell the remainder of his stock by auction.; that the sale realised £l4 Is. gross ; and that deducting the costs of sale anti his solicitor's costs he received the sum of £7 18s Bd. which was all the assets he had. —Bankrupt was examined At some length by Mr Johnson (assistant official receiver). He admitted that there was a loss of about £3OO in seven months. Asked why he had delayed earning to court, he said he was expecting some money which his former employer had agreed to pay him but that, woutld not have enabled him to 'clear off his liabilities. The weather had been so bad during the past summer that on many occasions he had nog been able to open his stalls at the various markets which he attended. The profits had barely.. covered his travelling expenses The examination was adjourned until October 4th;

THE FUNNIEST PLAY IN LONDON.

Visitors to London during the holiday season should not fail to see Sir Frank Burnand's most entertaining farce "Mrs. Ponderbury's Past" which is being per- formed eight times weekly at the Vaudeville, the handsome theatre in the centre of the Strand, managed by Messrs. A. and S. Gatt. Many years have passed since a farce was received s with such a chorus of unanimous approval by the public Press, but this is not to be wondered at, since, in addition to the mirthfulness of the entertain- ment, no pains have been spared to procure the best possible company for the performance of the piece. NIA% Weedon Grossmith has made a great success in the Dart of the adventurous Ponderbury ; Miss Marie Minton was never better suited than in the part of the strong-minded wife; and Miss Maudi Darrell, the popular musical comedy artist, who introduces a clever dance and the lively song "Molly Molyneux " (now one of the " rages" of London) obtains the utmost effect from the character of a music-ball "star." ' Owing to the success of "Mrs. Ponderbury's Past" matio4es vial be given every Wednesday and Satur- -1 day throughout the season.

WARRINGTON TOWN COUNCIL.

A SHORT MEETING. The Sankey Canal Bridge Scheme.

Twenty minutes sufficed for the transaction of the monthly meeting of the Warn ton Town Council, which was held at the Town Hall on Tuesday evening. The Mayor (Alderman Smethurst) presided, and there were also present :—Aldermen Bennett, 3 ohn Evans, NW J. Forshaw, D. Tinnion, Councillors T. H. Ashcroft, J. Ball, T. Barton, W. Benson, T. Burton, T. Chantler, W. flegg, J. G. Craik, D. S. Duncan, James Evans, L. Ellison, J. Lord, J. J. Nield, J. Pemberton, J. Skelland, T. S. Steel, F. Stott, 0. Toole, G. T. Wilding, J. Wilkinson, W. J. Wilson. 3. L. Whittle (town clerk), A. T. Hallaway (deputy town clerk), J. Fairhurst (borough treasurer), J. Dens (water engineer), 3. M. Murray (director of education), Dr. J. C. Hibbert (medical officer). The MAYOR appealed for a rapid transaction of the business, as some members of the Council had been engaged at the Local Government Board inquiry dnring the greater part of the day.

SUNDAY TRADING.

A letter was read before the Watch Committee from the Secretary of the Working Men's Mission Bank Quay, with reference to Sunday trading, and ft was resolved that the Chief Constable be requested to report on the matter. The minutes were agreed to, on the motion of the MAYOR, seconded by Councillor WILKINSON.

===== 0002601_19070907_art0085.txt | included=true | included =====
SCARLET FEVER.

Dr. Gaflikin having submitted a report with reference to an outbreak of scarlet fever in the town. the Health Committee resolved that Sankey Sanatorium be used as a convalescent hospital for patients from Aikia-street hospital. HONORARIUMS FOR EXTRA WORK.

The Health Committee recommended that an 'honorarium of 250 be granted to Dr. Gaffikin for extra duties discharged daring the illness, and since the death nf the late Dr. Gornall ; and that an honorarium of £25 be granted to Mr. W. T. Flood, chief inspector of nuisances, for the discharge of similar extra duties.

===== 0002601_19070907_art0086.txt | included=true | included =====
HAIRDRESSERS AND EARLY CLOSING.

The Inspector of Nuisances having reported the, result of his investigation on the petition of the Hair- dressers' Association for a closing order, it was resolved that in the opinion of this committee a prima facie case has been made out forsuch order. ' Councillor EVANS - moved, and Councillor BURTON seconded. the cimfirmation of the minutes. Councillor STeet, alluding to the question of the -hairdressers and early closing, asked if the order was made whether all hairdressrs would have to close at •eight, except on Saturday? The MAYOR: I suppose so. Councillor STEEL added that that would deal hardly with hairdressers living in the suburbs—Latchford, the Cemetery district and Sankey Bridges—who did little day business and depended on night customers. The DEPUTY TOWN CLERK explained that the com- mittee considered a prima facie case had been made out, but before an order could be made, there would be an inquiry at which the opposition would be heard. Councillor EVANS replied that the point raised by Conncillor Steel would be ventilated. The minutes were passed. A SEWERAGE QUESTION. LORD LILFORD'S OFFER. The Paving and Sewerage Committee reported that at a recent meeting the following proceedings of the sub, committee appointed to interview Mr. J. B. Selby (Lord Lilford's agent) were read "The sub-committee met Mr. Selby (Lord Lilford's agent), with regard to his objection to the paving of Longshaw-street. and after discussion he withdrew the objection. Mr. Selby brought before the sub-committee the question of the sewering of the land lying to the north of Polly-lane, and stated that if the committee provided a system of sewerage for that district he would give the Corporation a strip of land for the widening of Folly-lane. The sub-committee promised that the matter should have the early attention of the committee. The ToWN CLERK reported that objections to the paving of Longshaw-street (objection of J. B. Selby on behalf of Lord Lilford) had been withdrawn. The BOROUGH SURVEYOR submitted ordnance map for Folly-lane district and explained the present sewer- -age arrangements in that district :‘- Resolved, "That in order to enable the committee to expedite the con- sideration of a scheme of sewerage, Mr. J. B. Selby '(Lord Lilford's agent) be asked to furnish an estate plan of the land lying on the north of Folly-lane." Alderman Tinnotr in moving the adoption of the minutes, made special reference to the interview with Mr. Selby. He said the seworing of the land would involve the expenditure of a considerable sum of money, but until the committee had seen the plan in detail they had decided they could do nothing in the matter. Mr. Selby bad promised to furnish a plan. The expenditure was one that would have to be met; the committee had no option in the matter. A sewer must be put down where buildings were likely to be erected and it was always the desire of the committee to have such districts sewered and made as sanitary as possible. The committee, however, would do nothing before consulting the Council. SANKEY CANAL BRIDGE. AN IMPROVEMENT SCHEME.

The County Bridgemaster wrote invitine representa- tives of the Paving and Sewerage Committee to attend a conference at Preston, with reference to the improve- ment of Sankey Canal bride. The Chairman (Alder- man Tinnion),•Councillor Wilkinson and the Borough Surveyor attended the conference and the deputation drew up the following reuort— " At the outset ;Ir. Alderman TIN.NION informed the 'conference that whilst the Corporation of Warrington were in favour of the intprovement they understood that all the county authorities wished for was War- rineton's moral support and this they were prepared to give. The Chairman of the Main Roads Committee thanked him for the expression and went on to explain that whilst they were glad to have Warrington's support they hoped for something more, and had prepared a plan and scheme which, if carried out, would be greatly to Warrington's advantage. He further stated that the London and North-Western Railway Company who were primarily responsible for the bridge, 'bad raised no objection to the proposed improvement. It appeared that the cdunty authorities had drawn up a plan and scheme for anew bridge appor- tioning the cost thereof between the various authori- ties including- Warrington. whose estimated share was £750. Mr. Alderman Tinnion, during the discussion which took place, informed the conference that as this was the first intimation of the pro- posal to ask Warrington to contribute towards the improvement, be must at once state that he absolutely and definitely declined to take any responsibility, inasmuch as the Council had not previously been con- -suited in the matter, but if the plan and details were forwarded, the whole question would be considered by a committee of the Council, and this course the conference agreed to adopt." Resolved, that the action of the Chairman be approved, and that the best thanks of the committee be given to the deputation for their repoit. Alderman TINNION said that at the conference he took a position which he thought \ could get be prejudicial to the interests of the Corporation. It was fortunate that he did so for a scheme was being proposed that would cost the L. and N.-W. Railway Company, or someone else, from £3,000 to £4,000, so that the struc- ture could be equal tothe traffic of the present day. The 'County Council contended, and he thought rightly, that the L. and N.-W. Railway Company should be respon- sible for any expenditure in the direction he hao indicated, according to their Act of Parliament. He repudiated any liability on tbe part of tbe Corporation, and told them that if they were prepared to treat all bridees,within the borough or appreaching it particularly the Warrington Bridge, on the same lines, be, for one, could see no objection to the matter being considered if they would furnish proper plans and particulars to the right committee. Councillor CRAIK seconded. Councillor HAWTHORN suggested that if any agree- ment were come to about the bridge at Sankey it would be advisable to stipulate that the Corporation would have the right to construct tramways across it; as the district was destined to become en important one and the extension of tramways to Penketh-lane ends would -tend to develop it. Alderman TINNION replied that he did not think the committee had anything to do with the matter. They -could not construct tramways over it, as it was outside the borough. They might put a light railway, which was the same thing—(laughter —but not an ordinary tram. There was no doubt, however, that the com- mittee would bear the matter in mind, If they were going to get the Railway Company to put down a bridge free of costs to the borough and take on the additional matter of the tramways as well, he would be very glad. '(Laughter.) The minutes were approved.

INCREASES OF SALARY.

At a meeting of the Water Committee on August 12, it was resolved that the salary of Mr. James Gray be increased from £l3O to £l5O per annum. The General Purposes Committee, at a meeting held on August 26, resolved that the salary of Mr. T. H. Spinks, assistant town clerk, be increased by the sum of £26 per annum, namely, from £l5O to 4176. The increases were agreed to without comment. ST. ELPHIN'S PARK.

Among the documents submitted by the General Purposes Committee for the affixing of the common seal of the Corporation were the contract with the trustees of the Clergy Widows and Orphans Fund for the purchase of land, Manchester-road. The MAYOR moved the confirmation of the minutes.

Alderman BENNETT seconded, and expressed the satis- faction with which they were able, as a committee, to announce that the trustees of the Training College had agreed to their terms for the purchase of land near Manchester-road. It would be -a greet boon to the inhabitants of the district, and he hoped it would be a source of satisfaction to them to know that St.Elphin's Park was saved, and saved on terms which were satis- factary to practically the whole of the members of the Council. (Hear, hear.) The minutes were approved. GIFT TO THE MUSEUM.

1 The Museum Committee have resolved, "That the special thanks of the committee be given to Mr. J. Paul i Rylands, Mr. W. H. Rylands' and Miss M. G. Rylands for their gift of a marble bust of the late Thomas Glasebrook Rylands, by J. Warrington Wood, and other , portraits and specimens. Alderman BENNETT moved the confirmation of the minntes, and alluded to the handsome gift of Messrs. and Miss Rylands. The Committee were deeply indebted Ito them. The thanks of the Committee had also been 1 given to Mr. W. Sharp Ogden for his kindness in examining and describing the Roman coins found at Wilderspool. The result more than justified all that had been said with regard to the importance of the old Roman remains at Wilderspool. Councillor ELLISON seconded, and the motion was carried.

Resolved, " That the Town Hall, Parks and Baths Committee be invited to meet with a view to the question of the development of the land referred to being fully considered." i The minutes were approved. THE SHIP CANAL ACTION, I The Acting Conservator of the Mersey having asked the Mayor for an interview to discuss in a friendly spirit the best action for the Conservancy to take for the maintenance of the navigation of the river, without , causing undue interference with the interests of the manufacturers of Warrington, it was resolved, by the Rivers Committee :—" That the Acting Conservator be requested to meet the chairman of the committee at the conference."

Alderman BENNETT moved, and Alderman FORSHA.W I seconded, the passing of the minutes. Councillor HAWTHORN asked what was the position 'of the Corporation with regard to the costs of the action in the case of Oros&ld and the trades v. the Ship Canal Company. Alderman BENNarr replied that the matter was under consideration, and it would not, therefore, be wise to say anything about it. (Hear, hear.) I The-M/YOR : It has been reported that the matter has been settled without our cons eat, but that is not correct.

The minutes were then confirmed. 1 THE CHEMICAL FIRE ENGINE RETURNED. t At a meeting of the Fire Engine Committee, the sub- committee appointed to consider the advisability of obtaining expert evidence on the Chemical Fire Engine and escape reported that they had invited the superin- tendents of Bury and Stockport brigades to make an inspection and report. The reports of the experts were submitted. The Town Clerk submitted correspondence • which had taken place between himself and the solicitors for Messrs. Simonis and Co. and after fully consider- ing the same it was resolved that the Town Clerk be instructed to give to the makers notice to have the chemical engine and escape removed from the Corpora- tion's premises without further delay, otherwise the Corporation will send the same to the station for despatch at the makers' risk and • cost. —At a meeting en the 26th of August, the Town Clerk read correspondence which had taken place , between the solicitors to Messrs. Simonis and Co.. • Ltd., and himself with reference to the combined chemical engine and escape, and reported that he had, ' in accordance with the committee's instructions, des- patched the engine. After fully considering the matter it was resolved : "That the Town Clerk give Messrs. Simonis and Co. seven days within which to state • whether they are prepared, within three months, to supply a machine to comply strictly with the contract j entered into by them, as if not the committee will buy ; elsewhere."

The minutes were approved, the mover bang Coun- cillor BURTON, and the seconder Councillor BARTON.

PENKETH.

ANNIVERSARY SERMONS.—The anniversary sermons at the Penketh Wesleyan Chapel were preached at the above place of worship on Sunday. The Rev. E. J. Gawne took both services and preached two edifying sermons. The choir rendered special music at both services the anthem in the morning being "0 Worship the King," Miss Johnson sang the solo. In the evening when the choir gave the anthem "God who =lciest earth and Heaven," Miss E. Plumpton took the solo part. During the service Mr. H. Plumpton also sang a solo, "Behold I stand at the door." Both services were well attended. It was a pleasing feature to see so many church friends at both services, which prpves the harmony that exists between the two denominations at Penketh.

WESLEYAN GUILD RAMBLE.— On Saturday afternoon the Wesley Guild had their ramble, Lymm being the place chosen. Despite the inclemency of the weather there was a good turnout, which numbered 36. Lymm was reached in various ways by the party. Some cycling others going by train. On arriving they at once began to enjoy the features which Lymm is noted for, until five o'clock, when the party sat down to tea. After tea several of the party went to see the flower show which was being held. The Rev. E. J. Gawne accompanied the party, and on arriving back at Penketh, all were agreed that a very pleasant afternoon had been spent.

PADGATE.

A NEW BOOK ING OFFICE AT PADeIAT,E STATION.— Railway travellers between Warrington and Padgate will be pleased to bear that the Cheshire Lines Railway Co., are making a new booking office on the South side of the 'station. The work of construction has just been commenced. The booking office will meet a much felt want and will obviate the necessity of the passengers Warrington way of crosqing the bridge for a ticket and also stop the dangerous habit of crossing the lines. /

WARRINGTON

Independent Labour Party. Mr. H. Russell Smart, who had been announced to speak at Bridge Foot on Sunday evening wired to say that he could not come, because be had only, just arrived from Stuttgart and was detained in London on important business, but said he would come on ! Sunday, September 29th. There was a large audience; at Bridge Foot. Addresses on Socialism were delivered' by Messrs. J. Hampson and W. Pook of the local branch. Councillor Stott presided. Winwick Horticultural Society. The annual show promoted by this Society was held in the Schoolroom on Saturday, and, despite the unfavourable weather which has prevailed during the greater part of the season, some splendid exhibits were shown. The show was a great success in every way, and praise is due to the hon. secretary, Mr. J. E. Pettener, and the committee, which was composed of the following gentlemen :—Messrs. R. Jolley, G. Beard, W. Britch, G. Baker, O. Gerosa, Rutter, J. Walker, T. Johnson, A. Baker, R. Melling, and R. Robinson. A special exhibition of window plants, not for competition, lent by of Dr. Simpson, was greatly admired. There was also a splendid display of honey , and wax by Mr. Walker, of Hermitage Green. ! Choir Trip. ' . On Saturday, the adult members of the choir attached to the Church of the Sacred Heart had their annual trip to New Brighton. The party left the Central Station at ten minutes past one, and on arrival at Liverpool they proceeded to the landing stage and boarded the saloon steamer,Pathfinder,for a two hours channel cruise. Subsequently they crossed to New Brighton, where tea was served at the Criterion Restaurant. The Tower was afterwards visited, and the return journey was made from Liverpool at 10.50. Warrington was reached at 11.20. The outing was an enjoyable one. The thanks of the choir are due to Father Ryan for providing the outing and to the choir- master, Mr. John Gibbins, for the able manner in which he carried out the arrangements. KIEL BurrEß, Is. and is. ld.per lb. ASHLEY'S!—Advt Lymm _Flower Show. - - -

-The Lymm and District Horticultural Society held their first annual exhibition in a field off Maltman's- road, Lymm, lent by Mr. T. E. Milligan Grundy, on Saturday. Tliere was a Capital number of entries in all sections, and the quality of the exhibits was good. The weather was beautifully fine and a large number of the public attended. An excellent display of flowers, fruit, and vegetables was seen in all sections of the ',how. The cottagers' classes came out wonder- fully well, much better, than one would have anticipated for this season. A good standard was also attained by the amateurs, who showed a capital lot of vegetables. In the open classes there was keen competition all round. The collection of flowers would have done credit to any show. An outstanding feature was the display of begonias. During the afternoon an attractive musical programme was given in the showfield by the Band of the 3rd V.B. Lancashire Regiment and Warrington Male Voice Choir. Solos were also finely interpreted by Miss Florence Egerton. FOR PURE PORK SausAoz,try ASHLEY'S.—Advt. Band Contest at Belle Vue. Over 50,000 persons attended the annual brass band contest at Belle Vue, Manchester, on Monday. The bands who competed were :—Batley Old, Luton Red Cross Silver, Irwell Springs (Bacup), Hebden Bridge, Rochdale Public, Shaw (Oldham), Lea Mills (Matlock), Crosfield's Soap Works (Warrington), Pendleton Old, Good Shaw (Rawtenstall), Linthwaite, Wingates Temperance, Royal Oakley Silver (Blaeneau Festiniog), Lindley, Northfield (Birmingham), Black Dyke, Wyke, Pemberton Old, and Kettering Rifles. The test piece was a selection from Macfarren's opera, " Robin Hood," specially arranged by Lieutenant Charles Godfrey, and the judges were: Lieutenant Charles Godfrey, London; Mr. Manuel E. Bilton, bandmaster, Royal Horse Guards, London; and Mr. Frederick Vetter, musical director, Manchester. After a spirited competition, the first prize of £5O and the 50-guinea challenge Cup were awarded for the second time to Wingates Temperance Band, followed by: 2, Black Dike ; 3, Goodshaw ; 4, Royal Oakley (Blaenaii Festiniog) ; 5, Shaw; 6, Pemberton Old; 7, Hebden Bridge. Friendly Society Picnic. - - - - - - - - •

The annual picnic in connection with the Warring- ton branch of the Hearts of Oak Electoral Society took place on Saturday, when a visit was paid to Chester. The party was joined at Warrington by the District delegate, Mr. Rogers, of Widnes, and Mrs. Rogers. On reaching the ancient city all enjoyed a sail clown the Dee to Eccleston Ferry. After a stroll through the Eaton Hall Grounds, the party returned to Chester by boat and proceeded to the Holborn Cafe, 29, Foregate-street, where an excellent tea was provided and thoroughly enjoyed. The visitors were joined during tea by Mr. Wright, the late delegate for the Chester District. After tea the President (Mr. Kilduff) occupied the chair, and welcomed the Chester delegate, and also their own delegate and his wife, and expressed the hope that they would long be spared to meet the Warrington members of the Hearts of Oak Society, (Applause.) tioth delegates after- wards addressed the members, expressing the pleasure it had given them to attend such a happy gathering of the Warrington members. They hoped the outing would create many friendships, that would be of long standing in the future. (Applause.) Mr. Sparkes, an old member of the society in Warrington, proposed a vote of thanks to the delegates for attend- ing. Mr. T. Bate seconded, and the motion was carried with applause. After a ramble round the city the party returned to Warrington by the 9.10 train from Chester. GARDEN TENTS.—Tennis, Croquet, and other out- door sports.—.l. S. GARLAND, 51, Bridge-street, Warrington. -Advt. - - -

P.S.A. - Sunday being the first Sunday in the new half-year of the society there was an improved attendance at the Wycliffe P.S.A. meeting. The Rev. John Yonge presided, and said that while they were bound to admit that the number of members enrolled was the lowest in the history of the movement, it called for, special effort on the park of every member, which would. soon alter the existing state of things, and would soon make them numerically as strong as ever. It was the individual members who could do that. If 'everyone present did his best a grand result would soon he seen. In the absence of the advertised speaker Mr. Wynne-Jones took for his subject, "Habit." Great care Rho uld be exercised in the formation of habits. He dwelt particularly on the habits of" gambling and drinking." These Vad habits were, he said, formed by degrees, but slowly and surely spoiled .the life and character of the individual. Mr. W. H. Wallington sang two solos, " Lovest thou Me ?" and " The Lord is my Shepherd," in his most impressive style. Mr. W. 11. Payton presided at the organ. Mr. Joseph Owen and Mr. W. Beswick also took part in the meet- ing. Nine new members were enrolled, making a ! total of 380. —The first meeting of the new half-year ' in connection with the Buckley-street Society was held ilon Sunday. There was a large gathering of members and Mends. About 20 new members were enrolled. Mr. William Stephens, of Stockton Heath, presided, and gave an address to young people. Mr. William Shaw, the speaker of the afternoon, took for his subject " Being fit." The soloist was Miss Jennie Armstrong, who was heard to great advantage in her two solos. Miss M. Plinston was the accompanist, Mr. E. Sanders read the afternoon lesson. WOOD AfaNTErs,Kitchen Ranges end Fire Grates, largest stock in the district.— J. S. GARLAND, Iron- monger, Bridge-street, Warrington.—Advt. Rechabites' Picnic. The annual picnic of John Dimond and Wycliffe juvenile tents branches of Charity tent No. 11, took place on Saturday. Frodsham as usual was the place selected. The forenoon of Saturday foreshadowed a wet and miserable day, which somewhat marred the usual anticipation and damped the spirits not only of the children, but even the superintendents gravely shook their heads and predicted a wet day. Fortune how- ever, favoured the party and about an hour before the time the special train chartered to convey the party. was due to leave Bank Quay Station-1.45 the rain bad ceased and before the youngsters reached Frodsham there were hopeful signs that they were going to have a much better time than any of them had anticipated, the sun breaking through the clouds and bathing the bills in glorious sunshine. The after- noon was spent by the children in games of rounders, donkey riding, swing boats, a cricket match between the two juvenile tents causing no little "excitement amongst the aspirants to the game. John Dimond, the premier tent beat Wycliffe tent very easily. About 430 p.m. tea was prepared and about 430 children with a number of their parenta sat down. After tea the children again returned to their games whilst , others wended their way over the hills some visiting Dnnsdale Hollow and others the Caves. The return journey was made, the train leaving Frodsham about 8.30 p.m arriving at Warrington about 8.50 p.m. after a most enjoyable outing.

The Gymnasium. The Borough Gymnasium will be re-op2ned on Monday next. The session ends in May of next year. Anyone desirous of joining the classeg should apply to Mr. A. Mottram, the director of exercises. . Pearson & Knowles Coal & Iron Co., Ltd. The Directors have decided to recommend the payment of the usual half-year's dividend, at the rate of six per cent. per annum, on the preference shares, and a half-year's dividend at the rate of fifteen per cent. per annum, free of income tax, on the ordinary shares, making ten per cent. for the year. Fire at Messrs Parkinson's Mill. Shortly after half-past eight on Saturday evening a fire broke out at the mill of Mr. William Parkinson, corn merchant, in Bank-street. The alarm was given and the local brigade turned out with a full comple- ment of men. The fire engine escape and ladders were brought on the scene but it was found the latter were not required. The fire was soon extinguished. , The damage amounted to between £0 and SS which is covered by insurance. If the brigade had not turned out so promptly there would probably have been serious damage. The origin of the fire is unknown. SAUSAGE SEASON now commenced. ASHLEY'S—Advt. Music in the Park. A Drunken Motorist Fined.

At Chester, on Monday, Thomas Thornton Gregson, master baker, of Lymm, pleaded guilty to having been drunk whilst in charge of a motor-car on Grosvenor- road on Sunday.—Mr. Charles Francis Bell, of Norley Hall, said he was driving along Wrexham-road from! Llangollen on Sunday, and when he came to Pulford, he saw prisoner's car in front of him swerving from 1 side to side. Approaching two cyclists the car I swerved- towards them, but witness could not seal whether they were struck or not. A second cyclist was lying in the hedge as be (witness) passed, hut as the cyclist's friends were attending to him witness didi not stop, but proceeded to Chester where he warned the police, as he considered the driver was drunk.— I Captain Aikin, of the Chester Motor Garage, said that' on Sunday night he saw prisoner's car swerving from I side to side. Prisoner was the worse for drink, and I at the request of the police witness took charge of I the car.—Prisoner told the magistrates that he only had three drinks the whole of the day.—The Chairman said it was a very serious charge, as prisoner was endangering the lives of other people.—A fine of 20s. and costs or a month's imprisonment was imposed. Mr. A. J. King, M.P. and the House of Lords. Addressing a Liberal meeting at Eastham on Saturday, Mr. A. J. King, M.P. said there never was a more capable or zealous set of Ministers than those which comprised the present Government. In spite of that, they had to deal with a very effete piece of I machinery which moved slowly and creaked on its i hinges. Nevertheless, something had been done to remedy these defects, and the Grand Committees which had been established, and in which a large amount of useful work had been done, facilitated the business very much. The house was so overweighted with business that they would have to go in for some scheme of devolution with Irish, Scotch, Welsh, and English questions settled locally, leaving the House of Commons to deal with Imperial affairs. He was not sure whether their grievance against the House of Lords was greater because of their treatment of Liberal measures or of their inaction when the Tories were in office. If the House of Lords had exercised its revising functions when the Education Act of 1902 was introduced it would have been some justification for the action of the House of Lords now. If the Lords did not change their tactics, if they constantly thwarthed the wishes of the people as expressed by their representatives in the House of Commons, the time could not be far distant when the Government would have to appeal to the country to decide whether the will of the people or the will of the Lords should prevail. He hoped, when the time came, Cheshire would be as united in the cause of democracy as they were at the time of the last general election. (Applause.) TRAVELLING TRUNKS, DRESS BASKETS, &C. Try J. S. A smoking concert was held in the Parr Hall on Tuesday 'evening, under the auspices of the Warring- ton Football Club, to celebrate the cup victory and to inaugurate the forthcoming season. Ford, who made an excel- lent accompanist, opened the proceedings by a piano- forte solo. The Warrington Quartette Party were beard to advantage in the well-known song" Annie Laurie "and also in "A catastrophe." Three artistes from the Royal Theatre of Varieties—Mr. Cliff Ryland Mr. H. 0. Wills and Mr. V. Courtney— by the kind permission of Messrs.Johnson,Potter and Wilmdt,enter- tained the audience, Mr. Courtney's pianoforte sketches being particularly acceptable. Mr. G. Gough effectivly rendered two songs entitled "A sleep in the Deep" and " For King and Country." Messrs. Crovfield's Quartette Party were encored for two selections which were very tastefully played. Mr. J. Willett scored a success with Mona"' and "An evening song," and was heartily recalled, responding with" Sweet Marie." Mr. T. Rose greatly amused the audience with a humorcais song and sketch. Drowning Mystery., An inquest was conducted by Mr. R. Dobson at Stocktc,n Heath on Monday, touching the death of Alfred Blythe, 28 years of age, who resided with his father at 5, Liverpool-road, Warrington, and whose body was found in the Bridgwater Canal on Sunday morning.— Ssinuel Blythe, father of the deceased, said he last saw his son alive at nine o'clock the previousl Monday night. He had had a week's sickness and was suffering from lumbago. He did not seem in any way depressed. He understood that his son left home at about nine o'clock on Tuesday morning to go for a walk, saying that he would return for dinner.—ln answer to the Coroner, witness said deceased had lost one of his eyes, and had a false one. Sometimes he seemed to feel very sensitive about it.—Arthur Wilkinson, ironworker, 29, Heath-street, Stockton Heath, said that at 6.30 on i3unday morning he went to 6sh in the Bridgewater Canal near Red-lane Bridge. About 7.30 two men came up to him and said they had seen a peculiar object floating in the water about 200 yards away. He went back with them and cast his line over the object and drew• it to the side. It proved to be the body of the deceased. He then went, for the police, who returned with him and removed the body.—Acting-Sergeant Richard Bostockstationed at Stockton Heath, said he went to the spot with the last witness and removed the body to the Bridge Inn, Appleton. He examined the body, but found no marks of violence upon it. He thought the body would have been in the water about four days. He searched the clothing, and found a watch and chain, 3d. in copper, a pocket knife, and a book containing an address in Warrington.— The jury returned a verdict of "Found drowned."

PLEASANT PARAGRAPHS.

First youth (who thinks he is a born humorist) : "Gentlemen, let me introduce to you my friend, who isn't as stupid as he appears to be." Introduced Friend (without any hesitation): "That's precisely the cliffer- ence between my friend and myself."

Mrs. Smug '(looking wistfully after the partion) : "There's an odour of piety about that dear man." Mr. Smug: ."You never say that about me, yet parson and I both drink the same brand."

Clara's Young Man: "Won't you play some- thing for us, Maud?" Maud: "What shall it be?" Clara: "Oh, anything you can think of that's appropriate." Maud: "Then I'll play something soft."

Ethel (who is not famous for her good looks) : "I don't see why you should call Miss Whitmore plain. I'm sure I only wish I were half as good-looking as she is." Fred: "You are, Ethel—you know you are." And Fred is wondering whether he meant to acm- pliment her.

Dumley : "I met a fellow to-day who was simply crazy about a buried treasure; couldn't talk of anything else." Peckham: "That reminds me of my wife." Dungey: "Oh! Does she talk about one?" Peckham: "Yes, her first husband. I'm her sdcond. you know."

"We excel you in everything," boasted the American. "I don't think you do," responded the Britisher. "How about , our get-rich- quick concerns? Have you any to equal them?" ' "Certainly. How about our noble- men who marry American girls?"

"I'm going to take a stick to that miser- able beast of yours one of these days, madam." "I wish you would; neither his father nor myself can do a thing with him." "Madam, I am speaking of your dog, not of your little boy." "What! Take a, stick to my Fido ! You. horrid brute, you; don't you dare !"

Enthusiastic but Short-sighted Lady Ar- tist (to old labourer) : "My good man, what are those beautiful waving objects near those trees, rivalling the latter themselves in grace and beauty of outline, making such a beauti- ful variety in the landscape, and seeming to hang 'twist earth and heaven?" Old Labourer (gruffly) : "My shirts."

A young student who had gone to pass a University examination had the misfortune to be "plucked." By way of breaking the news to his parents he wrote home: "I have had a grand time here; everybody was good to me. The professors were especially kind, and asked me to come back again next term."

An Irishman looking for wOrk took his stand in a group at the gate of a large engi- neering establishment. By-and-by the fore- man came up to the gate and asked :—"Are there any drillers here?' "Yes," said Pat, stepping forward. He got the job at once, but he had not been working long at the machine when it broke down. The foreman, in anything but a pleasant mood, then in- guired : "Where' man, did you learn drill- ing?" "In the Militia," was Pat's reply.

"Mr. Scrimple," said the commercial mag- nate to the young barrister, "I want to make use of your valuable services." "Very well, sir," said Scrimple, as he gasped at the joy- ous prospect of a first brief. "What can I do for you?" "A firm which competes with my house," replied the magnate, firmly, about to bring an action for damages against me, and I want you to get them to enzage you as their counsel."

Over-shrewd lawyers often furnish their adversaries with weapons. "Did you see this tree that has been mentioned, by the roadside?" an advocate inquired. "Yes, sir; I saw it very plainly." "It was conspicu- ous, then?" The witness seemed puzzled by the new word. He repeated his former as- sertion. "What is the difference," sneered the lawyer, "between plain and conspicu- ous?" But he was hoist. on his own petard. The witness innocently answered: "I can see you plainly, sir, among the other lawyers, though you are not a bit conspicuous."

I seated myself on the corner of the table, and immediately from each. ,of the 16 doors a young man emerged, and the 16 lined up. The Governor said to them: "Your ser- vices are not required. Mr. Clemens is sitting on the bells." There was a cluster of 16 bell buttons on the corner of the table.

So you'll be leaving us, I pre- sume?" said his employer. "No, don't think I'd be so 'ard on you as that. But, you see, it's like this. My brother Dick, 'e got a job last week, to start yesterday, 'e was paid by the hour. Well, come yesterday, 'e was so queer 'e couldn't go ; and so, of course, I put on his clothes and went. We're twins, and I get paid here whether I work or not, and 'e don't. See?" Mr. Birchington saw.

The Chinaman who obtained] a licence to d* bu.sriness as an auctioneer is selling goods under the hammer in a store in Wuthfington- street, near Dupont. The place i€ fitted up on one side with a counter, behind which sits a Mongolian, wearing a skull-cap with a. red button. Before him he has an abacus to cast up accounts, and besides has sundry books in whith he keeps records of sales, making the entries with a cat's-hair brush dipped in Indian ink.

Behind him are a number of shelves stocked with gaudy-coloured shoes such as are worn by Chinese females and children, rare-coloured allk handkerdhiefs, small looking-glasses in teak-wood frames, chinaware decorated with pictures of animals known only to the Chinese, and which it would be impossible for the most expert white naturalists to classify, and fancy articles which please the Chinese senses. The auctioneer, in a light blue suit, was at his post, and was earnestly urging those in front of (him to purchase. "He says,", said the interpreter, "now you've got a chance to buy everything at your own price, and if you miss this opportunity you'll wish you never had been born, for you'll never get such a chance again." One Chinaman in the centre of the crowd draws the attention of the auctioneer to him; and with a long, bony finger pointed to a blouse on one of the cords. "He wants him to sell that," said the inter- preter.

The auctioneer produced the desired garment, and unfolded it after the manner of a cheap John auctioneer when offering for sale a red shirt, swearing that it will never fade, never shrink. and never lose a stitch. He dilated upon its quality and value, declaring that no Chinese merchant could afford to sell it for less than twenty dollars. Through the interpreter the reporter then ascertained what followed the offer of :the article.

"I will give fifty-five cents," said the man who had ordered the garment down. The auctioneer dropped the garment as if he had been struck by paralysis. A moment after he arose to his full height, and shading his eyes with his right hand, cast a glance at the man who had made the offer, and said— "Fifty-five cents! What is the matter? Are you crazy?" , "You said we could have the goods at our own price," said the bidder. "Yes, that's what you said," cried out a num_ ber of Chinamen from various parts of the MOM.

"But I did not say that you could have them for nothing," remarked the auctioneer. "Will anybody give any more for it?' Not a word was spoken for several seconds. "Now, here," said the auctioneer, holding up the garment in his left hand, and striking the counter with his right, "does anybody else offer any more for this; twenty dollar blouse?" One timid-looking individual in the rear of the place ventured to bid sixty cents., when he was assailed by the voices of at least twenty Chinamen, who exclaimed— "You have no business to bid more than this; man did! The auctioneer said any one could have, goods at his own price; this man fixed his price, and you have no business to inter- fere." (

The auctioneer entered into a long explana- tion as to the rights of bidders at an auoticin, but the crowd held him to his announcement that purchasers could have goods at their own price, and forced the second bidder to withdraw his bid.

"Give me the garment," said the first bidder, tendering two quarters and a nickel. "You cannot have it for that," said the auc- tioneer.

This assertion was followed by a jabbering by every one composing the crowd, which could only be compared to the chattering of a cage full of monkeys. The purport of the remarks was that the auctioneer had been taken at his word, and that he would have to let the bidder have the garment. He finally yielded, and passed over the blouse, and ,took the purchaser's money, which he handed to one of his assistants, who passed it over to the bookkeeper. The purchaser then attempted to fit on the blouse, but found it four sizes fob small for -him, and he then wa,nted the auctioneer to take it back and return him hits money; hut this he refused to do, saying he bought it at his own price, with his eyes open, and now he must be satisfied. The ,purchaser became wroth, denounced the auctioneer and the manner in which he did business, but found that the crowd which, but a few minutes before. had taken sides with him, had turned against him, saying— • "A bargain is a bargain, and anybody w'ho gets one must be satisfied." And uttering a few words, the purchaser of the garment sold it to a smaller man for thirty- five cents and went away. - -

THE REWARD OF MODESTY.

A Quaker who lived in a large country man- sion was in the habit of giving a book to each of his servants at the beginning of each year. One New Year's-day, as usual, he called hi.s servants together\ in his room, and told them to choose each one a book for himself. Among them was a little stableboy, who, when his turn came, went up to the gentleman, and said. "If you please, sir, will you. choose one for me, which,my mother can read too, for she is very lonely now my father is dead." The gen- tleman rose and took from his bookshelf a volume of the "Pilgrim's Progress," bound in leather, and packing it up told the boy to take it home to his mother. When they began look- ing through it. to their great surprise, out fell a five pound note. The boy's mother said there must be some mistake, so she sent her son off at once to the gentleman with it. The Quaker having heard the lad's story, said, "No, there is no mistake. I put that five pound note in for you, as a reward for your being so unsel- fish and modest." DAISY STOCKS.

A modest violet grew; Its stalk was bent. it hung its head,

As if to hide from view. And yet it was a lovely flow'r, its colours bright and fair; It .nli,sht have graced a rosy bow'r Instead of hiding thefe. Yet there it was content to bloom, In modest tints arrayed.; And there diffuse its sweet perfume

Within that silent shade. Then let me to the valley go, This pretty flow'r to see: That I may also learn to grow In sweet humility. ELIZABETH BREARLEY.

A VENERABLE TURTLE.

Mr. A. J. Case, of New York, is said to have a land turtle that was at its maturity in 1815. Mr. j Case's father, who had served as a colonel in the war of 181, found the turtle on his farm at Cutchogue, L. 1., and marked his name on its I under shell, "Benjamin Case, 1815." The first and last letters of the name are still legible. but the others have long since been obliterated.l In 1841, when Mr. Case was a young man work- ing on the farm, he, too, caught the turtle and scratched his name and the date, "A. J. Case,' 1841," right over his father's mark. This inscrip- tion is so plain that it can easily be read with- out glasses. The older one, except the date, re- quires a magnifier. Mr. Green, the present owner of Colonel Case's farm, found the turtle between 1841 and 1860, a,nd again a few days ago. The turtle is of the kind known among farmers as " skillpods." Its shell measures nearly six inches, and the body, when extended, over two inches more. The upper shell is nicely speckled with yellow and dark spots, but the left side ! has a curious wound, which, Mr. Case thinks. ' must have been made by a stroke of an old- , fashioned mowing scythe. The turtle's eyes look as bright as if it were only twenty years old, instead of, as it probably is, over one ; hundred.

HEALTH POWER

GOOD DIGESTIO

The power to think well, work well, sleep well, and enjoy life depends mainly upon the ability of your digestive organs to extract strength and nourish- ment from food. When digestion fails, as in dyspepsia and indigestion, the body is starved, no matter how much food is eaten. Your body is also poisoned, for food remaining in the stomach ferments, producing poisonous gases, which, being absorbed into the blood, shatter the nerves, dull the brain, create disease, and give rise to headaches, languor, loss of appetite, palpi- tation, flatulence, and other disorders of the blood and nerves.

When from errors in diet, climatic changes, or over- work, the stomach, liver and kidneys fail to perform their functions perfectly, there is no remedy that will so soon restore them to health and vigour as the tonic extract of roots and herbs, Mother Seigel's Syrup. As a digestive tonic and stomachic remedy it has no equal. This is the testimony of thousands NO:ppm it has cured of chronic iniigestion and dyspepsia. This was the case with Mrs. Gertrude Grimes, 29 Gertrude-road, Sprowston-road, Norwich. On February 25th, 190 T, she wrote :—"1 had not been well for a long time. I never could eat in the morning but felt dull and run down. Often there were pains at my stomach, and I was troubled with wind and headache. WARRINGTON BOARD OF GUARDIANS

The Deputation question.

A meeting of the Warrington Board of Guardians was held at the Workhouse on Wednesday under the presi- dency of Mr. W. Houghton. There were also present: —Mrs. Simpson, Mrs. Wilkinson, Miss Broadbent, Dr. Murray, Dr. Patterson, Rey. M. Ryan, Messrs. A. J. Jolley, J. Billing(); S. Davie', R. Bolton. W. Furness, W. C. Brooks, J. Evans, M. W. geton, G. Mather, J. Back. house, T. Ritchie, .T. C. Sutton (clerk), J. F. Awbery (master) and A. Wright (superintendent of Padgate Cottage Homes).

ROUSE COMMITTEE.

At a meeting of the House Committee Mrs. Frost's offer to entertain the children at Grappenhail was accepted with thanks. After lunch discussion and by the casting vote of the Chairtnan it was re3olved to instruct Messrs. Owen to prepare plans indicating the lines of drainage, main steam piping, electric cable and water pipes. The minutes were approved. AN APPOINTMENT. - -

The Local Government Board wrote sanctioning the appointment of Mrs. Hilditch as assistant mistress at Padgate Cottage Homes. • VACCINATION FERS. _ _ _

The CLERK said the Local Government Board had acknowledged his letter with reference to the fees it was proposed to pay for vaccination and promised to forward the necessary forms.

===== 0002601_19070907_art0107.txt | included=true | included =====
DEPUTATION QUESTION.

Mr. BILLINGE moved that the Board be represented at the meetings of the North-Western Poor Law Con- ference at Liverpool on September 27th and 28th by this Chairman, Vice-Chairman, Mr. Jolley and Mr. Mather (chairman of the Schools Committee). He remarked that they were honoured in having one of their colleagues (Mr. Jolley) selected to read an important paper at the Conference. Mr. SETON seconded. Mr. BouroN : Four seems a large num her. Mat is the usual number? . . . .

The CLERK said the usual number was three,lbut there was a double meeting at Manchester on,a double sub- ject at which Mr. Jolley was the only representative of the Board. While there, he was persuaded by the Con- ference people present to undertake the writing of a paper on a very important subject: "Contracts." It was therefore important that he should be one of the deputation. Mr. BOLTON objected to the number being increased to four and said the deputations seemed to consist of one class of gentlemen year after year. The CLERK said that was not correct, for although it waq the custom always to appoint the Chairman and Vice-Chairman the holders of those offices changed. Mr. BOLTON said some of the Guardians had not been on any deputations this year. There were some old Guardians who never got on one deputation, and it seemed a strange thing. The CtrairtmaN said the custom had always been for the Chairman, Vice-Chairman, and Clerk to attend, and he did not see why it should be departed from. Latterly they had included the Chairman of the Padgate Schools Committee, but he thought if they made a departure in that respect they should begin with the 'senior committee, which was the House Committee. The CLERK said he believed in connection with the Conference there would be a visit to tho training ship "Indefatigable," which would be interesting to the Schools Committee. •

The motion was agreed to. At this stage of the meeting :klr. Houghton had to leave, and the chair was taken by Hr._Jolley.

SALARIES INCREASED.

On the recommendation of House Committee it was decided that the salaries of the norter and portress be increased £5 each, making them £35 and £3O respec- tively. APPOINTMENT OF NURSE. . .

Miss Price %vas appointed charge nurse at the Infirmary at a salary of £3O per annum, increasing after one year's approved servic,4 to £32 104.. with the usual allowances of board, apartments, uniform &c.

USELESS SWIMMERS.

At an inquest held at Halifax on Monday on the body of a youth who had a tit in the Park-road Swimming Bath on Sunday morning and was drowned, it was stated that though the youth could be seen lying on the bottom of the bath none of half-a-dozen young men present would go in to get him out, though they were all good swimmers and one had gained prizes for diving. They said they were afraid and dare not go into the water. Eventually the body was got out by a railway labourer, who came in and dived in in his clothes.

Mrs. Maria 'Umber, for fifteen years mistress of Poulton Catholic School, was found dead on her bed on Monday in Poulton. EARS OF FOREST ANIMALS.

Within the limits of particular groups large ears may be taken, as a rule, says the Scientific American, to indicate either great powers of hearing or the necessity of catch- ing every wave or sound. Thus, forest-dwell- ing animals generally have much larger, and especially broader, ears than their relatives inhabiting open country. An excellent in- stance of this is afforded by the okapi of the Semliki forest, as corktrasted with the giraffe of the more open 1, districts of' Africa —the ears in the one case being excessively broad and leaf-like, while in the other they are comparatively narrow and pointed. Simi- larly Grevy's zebra, which inhabits scrub jungles in Somaliland and north-east Africa generally, has much larger and wider ears than the ordinary zebra of the open veldt.

IMMUNITY FROM SCORPION POISON. A remarkable case of immunity with re- spect to the stings of a species of scorpion in a Mexican Indian is recorded by Mr. Carl Lumholtz in his interesting book, "Un- known Mexico." This Indian could handle these animals in a wonderful way without being • stung. He would allow a small light- brown species to run up his sleeve, or twist it between his thumb and forefinger. The immunity seemed to depend on the method of treatment, for the Indian related that when he mistreated them, as he was apt to do when intoxicated, they did ' sting. For this, however, he had a remedy. He would simply cut open the scorpion, and apply it to the wound. And Mr. Lumholtz was in- formed that this remedy was commonly used along the west coast. To protect themselves against the evil effects of scorpion stings, again, some people would eat plenty of sweet potatoes. The use of the scorpion as an ANTIDOTE TO ITS OWN STING

may be compared to the use of serpent's venom as a remedy for their bite. It has been stated that in South Africa the natives were in the habit of catching a serpent, squeezing out the venom, and drinking it. This produced a sort of intoxication, but protected them from the effects of the bite. In other parts they are said to extract the poison glands from the snakes they kill, and drink the secretion. They appear thus to gain absolute immunity. And such state- ments receive some support from Professor Fraser's discovery that an animal receiving a dose of serpent's venom is thereby pro- tected against the deadly effects of such venom injected sub-cutaneously.

NERVE IMPULSES AND THEIR PROPAGATION.

In a paper on the p'ropagaVon of nerve im- pulse, published in the 4(merican Journal of Physiology, Mr. Sutherkind gives it as his opinion that the electrical properties a nerves have received much attention, and the present hypotheses of nerve impulse pro- pagation, though seemingly purely mechani- cal, are in reality to be regarded as elec- trical also. For though he refers the " con- ductivity " of -nerve to the rigidity of Its substance, he has previously given electrical explanations of cohesion and rigidity. Two lines of thought lead to a conception of the possible importance of rigidity in the phe- nomena of nerve and muscle. In the first place, it is known that a -telly offers but

LITTLE MORE RESISTANCE to the passage of a -small ion than does pure water at the same temperature, despite the enormous difference in the large - scale vis- cosities of the two media. This proves that in a jelly the molecules 'of the gelatine form a mesh dividing the jelly into compartments with network walls which confine the mole- cules of -water in batches. The cellular structure gives to the jelly its rigidity, yet the meshes are so open that an ion urged forward by electric force has little difficulty in passing from one compartment to another, and encounters most of its resistance in passing through the batches of water mole- cules. Just as an ion moves through the jelly almost independently of the presence of the network, there ought to be phenomena of the jelly confined to the network as re- gards

It seemed to Mr. Sutherland that muscular contraction and nerve conductivity might be physiological aLswers to this query. The second line of thought regards the slqwness of the propa- gation of. nerve impulse as probably con- nected with the smAll rigidity of the soft tissues in the animal body.

CURIOSITIES OF SIGHT.

It is. a fact not generally perceived by the majority of persons that if we run our eyes along the aide of a room, or along the horizon, the eyeballs do not follow the movement of the head with a smooth, uni- form motion, but keep in line by means of a series of quick, short,- almost Imperceptible jerks. At first one may not think that is so, but, 'says the New York Tribune, careful experiment will prove that it is. If the eyes are fixed on a moving object, then the =lion of the eyeballs in following the ob- ject is uniform; but when it is the head that moves, while the objects looked at remain 'fixed, the eyeballs perform in the manner just described. It is not necessary in order to observe this phenomenon that visible ob- jects should', be before the eyes. Let the eyes be shut, the fingers pressed lightly on the closed lids, and the head then turned from side to side. The little jerks with which tip - eyeballs follow the motion of the head will be distinctly perceived. Among the CURIOUS SCIENTIFIC EXPERIMENTS

that have 'been made in the effort to dis- cover the meaning of this peculiarity of the eyes is one in which a turn-table, large enough for a man to sit comfortably upon, plays a principal part. When the table is turned with a regular motion, the person sitting upon it, having closed his eyes, feels his eyeballs jerking as they follow the revo- lution of his head; but as the table con- tinues to turn the jerking gradually ceases, and at the same time .the person imagines that the revolution • is becoming slower, until at last, when the eyeballs no longer twitch, the table seems to him to have stopped turn- ing. If then, the table is suddenly 'bade to turn . faster, the jerking sensation is re- newed ; and if, on the contrary, it is made to turn moi:e slowly, the same senSp.tion experienced. But IIQW the person imagines that lie is being • revolved in, a direction opposite to that of the actual motion. Still more extraordinary is

THR EFFECT PRODUCED

by continuing the revolution, at a uniform rate after the person has lost the sense of rotation, and then causing him to bow his head upon his breast so that its motion is round an axis running from the chin to the back of the head. The instant this experi- ment is tried the person feels as if his head was turning round both of the , axes at the aame time, and the sensation is described as both curious and alarming. Some physio- logists believe that we possess a special sense relating to the rotary motion of thn! body. and they think that the °rutin of this sense is in tne ianyrintn of the ear. To tins organ they attribute our powers of equili- '3rium, so that, if it is seriously disturbed As reel and totter.

PASSIVE RESISTER'S UNKNOWN FRIEND. Mr. H. G. C. Allgood, the Liberal agent of Cardiff, who was taken to prison on Saturday for non-payment of the education,rate, was liberated from Cardiff gaol on Monday morning, when he was informed that the tine and costs had been paid and that he could no longer be detained. Mr. Allgood is quite in the dark as to who paid the money. The only information that can be gained from the prison authorities is that it was paid by a woman.

NOTICE TO CORRESPONDENTS. „Authors of letters intended for publication must in au eases send their names and addresses to the Editor as ,a guarantee of good faith. L'lutter intended for publication must be written on one side only of the paper and sent in, if possible,not ater than Thursday.

===== 0002601_19070907_art0131.txt | included=true | included =====
Betting at Sports.

Sir,—l am very pleased to hear that there are some people in this town of the same opinion as myself, for I am now alluding to the letter from one of your correspondents about betting at sports. I think it is a downright shame that betting should be carried on at the Sports, for surely we have enough misery, as I call it,when the news-boys are shouting out the first winners. I think this town and many others would be a great deal brighter if betting was stopped altogether, but this I think would be impossible. I know of many a case wheat) money has been lost after trying again and again to win, but never succeeding. One case especially,where it has been going on for months upon months, and now it Is too late to check remain, yours truly, "ONE WHO KNOWS."

===== 0002601_19070907_art0132.txt | included=true | included =====
Drunkenness in Warrington.

Sir,—Your article in the issue of Saturday must, I feel sure, appeal to all who have the cause of Temper- ance and the welfare of our fellow-townspeople at heart. Whether this appealing blight is on the increase is somewhat difficult to ascertain. For to go to the annual statistics relating to the arrests and convictions for drunkenness would be practically useless. For every one arrestel scores, yes hundreds, go scot free. We cannot blame the hot weather, so we may therefore assume that the people are extending their sympathies in a practical manner towards "The Allied Brewery Traders' Association of Great Britain," whose alarming poster now occupies such a conspicuous position in our local hostelries. The "threatened Licensing Legisla- tion" (say they) "means robbery and confiscation," while Guinesses, by the way, are now paying 25 per cent. "Thousands will be out of employ- ment," and "thousands, of course, will starve." I y, I wonder, didn't they say millions? If the £167,000,000 spent annually on drink was turned into other industrial channels, unemployment would cease, and starvation would be for ever blotted out of the pages of our history. They say "millions of , capital would be lost." Not lost but gone before. They pleadingly ask the public in big bold black letters to "Awake." This I thank God they are undoubtedly doing and the "Allied Brewery Traders" can do no other than admit however painfully, that is owing to the trend of public opinion that they find themselves in such a perilous position. Unfortunately they have a peculiar knack of crying out before they are hurt. Perhaps they have just realized the secure position in which the 190 i Act placed them. You refer to the scenes which confront us on a Sunday night most assuredly in this, the town of War- rington bears a terrible record. It is possible to stand on a certain street corner from 10 to 10.15 on a Sunday evening and count from 100 to 150 people of both sexes, young and old, under the influence of drink. Their breath is a sickening stench and their language too filthy to describe. The majority of townspeople make a practice of trying to get home before the pubs close" and are, therefore, probably i4norant of the terrible drink drama which is enacted in a so-called Christian country on the day which God has set apart for the spiritual rest of all mankind—public house servants included. We denounce the continental sabbath but allow a state of things to exist in our own land which would justly call forth a bitter torrent of scathing criticism from " our brethren over the channel." It seems hard to believe that four little children each under four years of age can be turned out for the day by their parents in Beautiful Warrington on a Sunday morning with faces caked with filth,rags for clothing and in a famished condition. They are found the same evening in the Queen's Gardens partaking of the sole meals of the day which consists of Banana skins picked up off the streets. Towering in the air above ft the noble statute to the brave men who sacrificed their lives for their King and country in South Africa below is the ghastly scene of pure young lives being shamefully sacrificed for a pint of beer. Surely the "Cry of the Children " finds an echo in Warrington, When, I ask, shall that echo have the response it demands? When can we say "The Church's one foundation is not the beer barrel' and our laymen cease to say" Amen" to the diatribes of the drink trade promoters. It is as a running sore on the body politic, a check on the polif:e, a blight to trade and commercial enterprise, and a standing menace to Christianity. Will every man rise up and shake off the fetters which bind him to a trade against which the whole instincts of humanity cry out.—l am,yours Moore, sth September, 1907.

J. R. GRIFFITHS.

===== 0002601_19070907_art0133.txt | included=true | included =====
LOCAL WEDDING.

Bowman—Smith.

There was a very pretty wedding at Cairo-street Unitarian Church on Tuesday, when Mr. George Bowman, son of Mr. William Bowman, Motherwell, N. 8., was married to Miss Janet May Smith, 'eldest daughter of Mr. Joseph Thomas Smith, 6S, Bewsey- street, Warrington. The bride and bridegroom are well known in the town, Mr. Bowman being fte manager of Messrs. Monks, Hall and Co.'s tube works, and a large congregation assembled to witness the ceremony. The Rev. W. E. Williams was the officiating minister. The bride, who was given away by her father, was charmingly attired in fancy grey silk eolienne trimmed with lace and silk. She wore a white felt hat trimmed with white and heliotrope chrysanthemums and carried a lovely shower bouquet of white roses and white heather. Her ornament was a pearl brooch-pendant, the gift of the bridegroom. The bridesmaid was Miss Mary Smith, sister of the bride, who wore a pretty dress of pale blue eolienne trimmed with lace, and a white felt hat trimmed with pale blue silk. Her shower bouquet was composed of pink carnations and maiden hair fern. Mr. William Taylor discharged the duties of best man. Mr. J. Muir presided at the organ and played appropriate selections including Meadelssohn's Wedding March. After the ceremony the reception was held at the residence of the the bride's parents, and the wedding party was photo- graphed by Mr. T. Birtles. Later in the day the happy couple left for Whitby where the honeymoon is being spent. The bride's travelling dress was a blue costume with hat to match. Bouquet of Wild Flowers.-1, Mabel Huelin ; 2, Fred Wickham ; 3, Phyllis Burrows and Frances Ray; 4, Alec Plumpton ; h c, Elsie Whitfieldand Alfred Lee; c, Edith Hinstone. • SCHOOL CHILDREN'S COMPETITION.

The CHAIRMAN said one of the principal objects of the Society was to encourage in the homes of the people the growth of beauty in the shape of plants and flowers. He •was happy to say that the number of entries was considerably larger than last year and he felt sure the visitors would agree that considering the place in which they lived and the disadvantages under which they necessarily laboured the show was a very creditable one indeed. (Applause.) The plants which they saw around them came from the small front gardens, from the small back gardens, from the little cheap greenhouse and the small frame, from the window in which a child had nursed a plant, from the lanes in which the young people had gathered wild flowers and from the schools in which the flowers had been culti- vated. When they considered the surroundings and the difficulties of growing flowers under those conditions, they would feel that the show was very encouraging to lovers of flowers.
