, attended to. There were, however, several points still needing attention, and more particularly would they emphasise the importance of early and correct staking. They trusted the teachers would do all they could to keep the enthusiasm of the children up to the necessary level and they would further suggest to the

committee the desirability of supplying -Plants at a much earlier period, as they thought the plants should be in the possession of the children at least six months before being submitted to the judges. Canon STEVENSON then announced the winners of the

competition for outside exhibits. Continuing, he WI that what was most encouraging to them in the work of the Society was that they had been taught by their experience that it was possible, even in such a town as that in wnich they lived, to do a great deal. It would be a most fatal thing if they were to fold their arms and say that Warrington was not a place in which they could grow flowers. That idea was contradicted by the exhibition which they saw around them, and he hoped they would be encouraged to go forward in their work of beautifying the town. In welcoming Mrs. Lyon, Canon Stevenson gaid they all knew the great interest she took in movements of that kind—in fact in everything which was for the benefit of the good old town of War- rington. (Applause.) Mrs. LYON said it was with great pleasure that she acceded to the kind wish of their secretary that she should open the exhibition. It was as they had heard, the second one of its kind which had been organised by their society and she was sure it must be a very success- ful one, from all she could see of it. Few movements had so entirely enlisted her sympathy as that attempt to help others to the enjoyment of what they might call the more poetic side of life, and it was a great pleasure to try and bring some colour into the lives of those who of necessity must spend their days in rather colourless surroundings. (Applause.) Our dear country, so highly favoured in other respects could not boast of a climate—at any rate, the least said about it this year the better—(laughter)---and at all times our northern regions required all the brightening up we could give them. The Society was helpful in three direction;. First it provided them with something nice to look at —and she was talking not only of that Society but of their kindred society in Stockton Heath. She wondered if people really realised the refreshment it was to tired nerves to have something harmonious and bright for their eyes to rest upon. When she drove into Warrington and passed through those avenues of well-cared-for and well-grown trees and saw those pretty gardens and the houses covered with creepers and decoted with window boxes, glowing with scarlet geraniums and other cheerful flowers, it soothed and rested her indescribably and she was sure it would have the same refreshing effect on other tired people. The work of the Society must in the second place give to many a fresh interest in life. What would life be to most of them without a hobby—and when people took to the gardening hobby they did ride it hard.(Laughter.) Last but not least, what sociability arose from these horticultural tastes ; what comparing of front and back gardens, what advice sought for and given amongst neighbours as to the best way to make things grow— and she had rever known any harm come from garden rivalries. The children also were not forgotten. She had been looking at those beantl- ful plants and she was perfectly astonished with what she saw. it Was very delightful to know how successful the juvenile section was and how the teachers threw themselves so heartily into the work. God bad given us a very beautiful world and it was our place to try and keep it so, as far as might be. When they reflected on the charm and historic interest of old Warrington with its then silvery river, witl its nice old black and white houses leading up to its old church, it did seem worth while to try and make it as nice as they possibly could in these altered circumstances. (Applause.) Therefore she felt emboldened to ask for more subscribers to the funds of the Society which oromised—and she believed would achieve its object— to make this dear old town once more beautiful War- I rington. (Applause.) The 111‘mia. in proposing a vote of thanks to Mrs. Lyon, said that during the year the Society had made great strides and he hoped it would continue to pro- gress. He was told that was the grandest display of flowers seen in Warrington for twenty years,so that they bad accomplished something. They were all glad to hear of the success of the children's section, but there was still a necessity of teaching children not to rob gardens, as he knew depredations of that kind went on. Alderman BENNETT seconded, and said the Society, could not have a better chairman and officials. Com- menting upon Mrs. Lyons' admirable speech, the Alder- man said that having an antiquarian bias, he was especi- ally interested in the remarks she bad made with refer- ence to old %%Torrington, and be sighed for a recurrence of those simple old times, which in many ways were more satisfactory than the bustling times in which they now lived. One of the objects of the Society was to try to inspire a love for that simple beauty with which former generations were familiar, and the Society could do a great deal in that direction. (Hear, hear, and ' applause.) The town, after the two years' work of the Society, was still anything but beantiful, and in many places intolerably ugly. They wanted to create in the community a loathing of ugliness which would influence their lives and surroundings. The people would then learn to love the beauties of natnre, the flowers and the trees, and one of the immediate results of their efforts would be a fostering of the love for the benutiful in the people, which would help forward the realization of a dream of "The City Beautiful." (Applause.) The vote was heartily accorded. THE MUSIC. Visitors to the show were afforded a musical treat, an orchestra, under the conductorship of Mr. Tom Baothroyd, submitting an admirable programme of classical and operatic selections. fixtures has been left in the hands of a committee of management, and now before a dab can qualify for the League championship it must play four clubs out- side its own section. In Lancashire there are eleven clubs, against twelve in Yorkshire, so that the minimum number of games which will entitle a Lancashire club to take part in the League champion- ship will be 30, against 3.2 for a Yorkshire club. There is no restriction put upon any of the clubs as to the maximum number of games they can play during the season. The League championship will be decided as before, by percentage of points, and there are still four top positions to strive for. A fter the end of March the four leading clubs will carry on the Leave as a miniature cup competition, exactly on the same lines as those of last year.

At Hull on Thursday, before a large crowd, Hull beat York by 18 points to 6. Wallace, one of the home team, was badly hurt.

RUGBY FOOTBALL.

By "()vast."

There should be a fine crowd at Wilderspool this afternoon to witness the opening match of the season with our neighbours from Leigh. A. year ago Warrington drew the opening match on the Leigh ground, and subsequently beat Leigh twice at Wilderspool by 31-18 3nd 15-8. The Cup-holders should have little difficulty in adding another victory to-day. They are usually just a bit too good for the Mather-lane men, though the points have generally to be keenly fought for.

Warrington will be short of the services of useful men like llockenhall and Jenkins to-day. The former is still suffering from an injured leg and Jenkins has not yet returned from the land of the leek. A. strong team has been chosen, however, and as Leigh will also suffer from absentees there should be no doubt of the local men's success.

Warrington's team will be : Tilley, full back; Fish, Isherwood, Taylor and Dickenson, threequarters; Lees and Brooks, half backs ; Boardman, Thomas, Naylor, Belton, Heath and Harmer, forwards.

Leigh will be handicapped owing to the absence from their team of Ellis Clarkson and ' Jimmy " Molyneux. The full back had not, at the time of writing, come to terms with the directors, and in his stead, Dick Higson, who performed meritoriously on several occasions last season, has been selected. Molyneux has hurt his ankle in the practice, and he will be substituted by Oliver Stirrup, a promising half back from Leigh Shamrocks. 'Aitchison, the ex-Salford man, who was expected to turn out for Leigh, has met with an accident whilst following his employment, and will also be an absentee. Leigh's selected team accordingly is: —R. Higson, full back ; W.Smith, Tom Johnson, H. Roscoe, and R. H.Neville, threequarters; S. Whittaker and 0. Stirrup,half backs; P. O'Neil, captain, J. Gallop, J. H. Blackburn, W. Winstanley, T. Shaughessy, and W. Webster, forwards. Reserve, J. O'Neil, forward.

Fish commences his tenth season to-day, and it is worth while pointing out that, notwithstanding a broken arm, he scored more points last season than ever before. There is no doubt about his value to the team when be is in condition. rhope he will have more luck this year than last and give the Club his best services. His scores in each season with the Warrington Club are as follow : ' Goals Tries Pts. 1898-9 1899-1900 1900-1 1901-2 1902-S 1903-4 1904-5 1905-6 1906-7

The "A" team do not begin till next week, when they will be at home to Rochdale Hornets.

The first Lancashire County trial match of the season will be played at Warrington on Wednesday the 25th inst.

The most interesting topic in Northern Union circles just now is the New Zealand visit. Our visitors are on the sea, and still the public h no wiser as to the composition of the team. Mr. Platt, the hon. secretary of the Union, has had no definite information since tha men started on their long journey and like everyone else is anxious to be know- ing who they are.

Some interesting information is, however, to hand from the other Hide, for the "Sporting Chronicle "of Wednesday publishes a long letter from a well-known Rugby football expert, the communication being dated Auckland, Julyl9. One of the mot interesting items is that one of the players will be G. W. Smith, and it is possible he may captain the side. All who saw the All Blacks when they were here before will remember this brilliant threequarter and his wonderful runs. Possibly the greatest game he played was against Scotland, and the last few minutes of that game will long be remembered by those who saw it. .Time was nearly up and Scotland were leading, when Smith got his chance. Down the wing he flew straight for the goal line, beat young Sepular, and turned what looked a certain defeat into a victory. That was his secoad try of the match.

The New Zealand correspondent, who speaks with considerable authority, states that the side will consist if 25 players-13 forwards and 12 backs. He goes on to say : This is the selection at present, having been made only last week, but there is a possibility of two more players being included bafore the team leaves. Three of the forwards at least will be general utility men, capable of occupying any position in the scrummage or rearguard. Nine have donned the New Zealand jersey, and several of these are All Black players. On good authority I have it that the selec- tion committee rejected three of the All Blacks who offered."

The same writer states that the team will be equally as strong as the All Blacks. He persuaded Smith to give his opinions of what will happen, and the crack three-quarter and hurdler writes : "He had a good idea of the calibre of the Northern Union players, and knew personally all the New Zealanders who were going. They. Will meet with the success the All Blabks did,' said Smith. They had suffi ient men to stand the tour. 'I would have liked to have seen the All Blacks play a Northern Union team or teams just to prove the assertion, but the margin of victory would not have been near so great. There is no doubt whatever in my mind that the All Blacks would have met with the same success so far as wins are concerned.' Ttat the Northern Union teams will halie hard nuts to crack I have no doubt, and the measure of their success will be watched with the keenest interest in New Zealand, as the people will forget whether they are professionals or amateurs so long as they do their lx.tst to uphold the honour of the Fern."

Mr. Platt does not expect the letter containing the names of the .visiting players for nearly another fort- night, so that the public will have to possess their souls in patience a little longer. The information given above, however, seems to prove that a repre- sentative side is coming, notwithstanding the barring clauso and signed agreements.

Further information is gradually coming to hand about tne New Zealanders. It seems that they intro- duced the virus of professionalism in Australia while en route for England. Three test matches were played one watched by 50,000 people, and the New Zealanders won all three. Northern Unionists are already looking forward to the time when they will be entertaining professional teams from Australia and South Africa as well as frorn New Zealand.

I, In view of the opening of the season to-day it is as well, perhaps, to con over the new rules. This year the Northern Union Committee have made only one important alteration in the laws of the game. Last 1 year's additions, such as "the reduction of players," " play the ball," and " touch play," have proved bene- ficial to the game, and they have been allowed to remain f,r another season. The working of the I " knock-on "rule does net appear to have given entire ; satisfaction, as it provided players with too much latitude and got them into a slovenly way of fielding . the ball. This the Committee evidently wished to obviate, so they have reverted to the old " knock-on " ' rule, which compels a player to take the ball cleanly and thereby improve the game generally.

-- In another direction, however, there have been many important changes. Two new separate Leagues have been formed, one for the Lancashire clubs and another for the Yorkshire clubs. Briefly, the position is thus :—There will be three Leagues altogether with separAte cups and medals, one for Lancashire, another for Yorkshire, and a General League for all the clubs. In the two first-mentioned, the games will count in the General League only. In addition there will be the competitions for the Lancashire and Yorkshire Senior cups played on the knock-out principle_, and in the latter part of the season the interest will be main- tained by the usual competition for the Northern Union Cup.

The principal alteration has been in the Leagu3 championship. This time all the arrangement o

Cricket.

Warrington were easily disposed of on Saturday, the victors being Wigan. Warrinzton were all out for 73, whilst Wigan responded with 85 for 9 wickets. The day began with a drizzly rain, and hopes of sport sank to zero. At about one o'clock, however, the weather changed completely, and the game was played under very pleasant conditions.

In the Warrington and District League St. Jame' suffered defeat at the hands of Orford Barracks. They were all dismissed for 121 and the soldiers knocked up 152 for 8 wickets. C. [dolt, for the losers, made the tpp score of the match, placing 43 to his credit. S. M. Jones (Barracks) knocked up 35 in fine style. The result of this encounter places St. James' and Cheshire Lines on equal terms and a match will be played between them to deoide the championship.

On Thursday, September 19th, a match will be played on the Arpley ground between the Guardians and officials of the Warrington Union and the Warrington Club. The Guardians will have the assistance of Frank Harry, of the Lancashire County team. Padgate Schools Baud will be in attendance and play selections of music during the afternoon. The proceeds of the match will be towards providing the annual prizes for the Padgate Industrial School children. Prices :is. at the Pavilion end and 3d. elsewhere.

Warrington Thursday accomplished a smart perform- ance on Thursday afternoon when they entertained Garston Police. The" bobbies " were put out for 41, and Thursday knocked up 129. Podmore made 51 in dashing style (8 fours) and then retired, L. H. Foster and P. Knight adding 25 and 14 respectively to the total.

GOING TO A WEDGING. Warrington Chauffeur Fined for Reckless Driving.

James Swinton, chauffeur to Mr. li. P. Densham, of Orford House, Warrington, WAS charged at Conway on Monday with drivir.g his car to the danger of the public at Towyn and Llandudno Junction on August 21st. Mr. .lames Marks prosecuted behalf of the police, and Mr. R. S. Chamberlain appeared for the de fence.

Mr. Marks said Dr. Prichard, Mayor of Conway, was being driven on the main road from Llandudno Junction to Tovvyn, and near the point where the Albert-drive joined the main road the coachman saw a car coming from the direction of Llandudno. The motor approached at a great spoed, and when nearing the horse it swerved slightly and the 'mare was struck on its right side, it being wounded, and the shift of the brougham being smashed. At this point the road was 22 feet wide, and there was also the width of the entrance to Albert- driva, making a total width of some 5G feet. John T. Parry, the Mayor's coachman, gave evidence and in cross-examination, denied that his mare was made restive by a train passing on the adjoining rail- way ; she was quite quiet until she thought the motor was. running into her. _

The evide-nce of the coachman was confirmed by two Birmingham men staying at the Tynycoed Convalescent Rome, and by a resident in the district, who were eye- witnesses. One of the witnesses thought that the car after the collision was travelling faster than any car he ever saw travel.

Mr. Chamberlain said his client's version was that the accident was caused entirely by the horse being startled by a train passing it at the same time as the motor car, and that, practically without warning, the animal reared on its hind legs, swerved to the right, and fell with its fore feet on to the car.

The defendant said be approached the doctor's brougham carefully,and at a speed of ten miles an hour. When the animal reared and plunged towards him he threw open the throttle of the engines and the car leaped forward. If he had not done that he believed that either himself or his passengers would have been struck with the horse's roofs.—Cross-examined, defen- dant slid he was driving to Frodsham, and wished to be there by a quarter-past two o'clock. A lady of the party wished to attend a wedding at Frodsham, but it was not very particular. Mr. Chamberlain : You would not have to scorch to get to Frodsham in 31 hours ?—Defendant: Certainly not. In answer to a further question, he admitted that some time ago his car injured a woman near Rhos, but that was due to a breakdown of the gear. A lady from Cork, called for the defence, slid if there had been no engine passing on the railway there would have been no accident.

Mrs. Densham, wife of the owner of the car, said that the car, driven by steam, ran very quietly. As the car and'brougharn met, they were on their correct sides, but the horse in rearing bridged the distance between the vehicles.—ln reply to Mr. Marks, witness said that she wished to get to the wedding, but there was no hurry. They were a little late in starting, but they did not race.

The Justices inspected Mr. Densham's car, and after a consultation the Court convicted the defendant of driving in a manner dangerous to the public, and fined him -£lO. With the costs and expenses the total came to £l6, which Mr. Densham paid.

ROYAL COURT THEATRE.

"The Walls of Jericho."

An exceptional treat has been provided for Warring- ton playgoers this week in the visit of Mr. Arthur Bouchier's Company with "The Walls of Jericho "—the powerful play written by Mr. Alfred Sutro as an expose of the "Smart Set." Our columns last week described the play, but we might just add here that it centres round Jack Frobisher, a Queensland millionaire and his pretty wife, Lady Alethea, daughter of the Marquis of Steventon and a leading member of the Smart Set which is devoted to bridge, scandal, cigarettes and an easy morality. For a time, Frobisher endures the misery of his wife's repeated absences from home, her neglect of their child, and her flippant 1 treatment of his remonstrances against her flirtations ' with Harry Dallas, a typical beau. Then the man in him comes to the front, as it always did in a Queensland crisis, and in a fierce and bitter speech he denounces. his wife's loose set to her face and tells her this mode of life must end. Being a spirited woman she resents the fierce invective which Frobisher pours upon her in his passion, and the wreck of two lives at least is imminent. Finally, Lady Alethea's better nature triumphs over her pride and the concluding scene is a happy one. We need hardly say that the play is most powerfully written and abounds in epigrams and smart sayings which tickle the palate of an audience. The company is an exceedingly strong one, and presents Mr. Sntro's masterpiece in capital style. Mr. Wilfred E. Payne, as Jack Frobisher, is magnificent, and his acting reveals fine dramatic power in Act 3. when he opens the vials of his wrath in the famous invective against the Smart Set. Lady Alethea, in the person of Miss Dora Hole, is piquant and charming throughout, while Miss Elsie Ross' as her sister the Lady Lucy, compels our admira- tion by her naturalness and vivacity. Miss Margaret Murray (the Frobishers' good angel), Miss Margaret Nvbloc. Miss Wynne Hanley, and Miss Sybil Brooke, all. leading ladies of the Smart Set, fill their parts artistically. Mr. Spencer Geach, as Hankey Bannister, and Mr. Kingsford Pearce as the haughty but impecunious Marquis, both give clever renderings of rather difficult parts. Mr. Charles Fancourt, as the polished debonair " villain " prepared to trifle with Lady Alethea's affections, has not an enviable role but he acquits himself in a manner which betokens the skilled actor. In fact we might go through the cast seriatim and distribute praise al / ib with full justice, but instead we would advise our readers—those who have not already helped to throng the house—to visit the R)yal Court Theatre to-night and witness the concluding perform- ance of "The Walls of Jericho "—as spicy and as clever a play as has yet been billed in the town. • NEXT WEEK'S PI.ZOGRInME.

Next week Mr. Frank Bertram and Company will pay a welcome return visit in the domestic drama " The Convicts Daughter." The play is now in its fifth year of tour and is immensely popular. Mr. Frank Bertram again appears in the leading role of Michael Graham, the tramp, which he has now played over 2,000 timer In the third act a sensational scene is witnessed, the convict jumping on to a moving train and escaping from prison. There is a wealth of beautiful scenery and effects and the performances should attract crowded audiences.

PADGATE GARDEN FETE. Successful Function.

The pretty Vicarage grounds at Padgate, at present bright with choice blooms, were on Thursday afternoon the scene of the annual garden f6te in connection with Christ Church. The weather favoured the event, for although about the opening time the outlook was unpromising, the threatening clouds dispersed, and for the rest of the afternoon there was pleasant sunshine. As a consequence there was a constant stream of visitors who spent an enjoyable time amid ideal sur- roundings. Admirable arrangements had been made,the ladies throwing themselves into the work with such energy as to cause one of the churchwardens in his remarks at the opening ceremony to exhort the male kind to emulate their example. In several marquees there were stalls on which all kinds of useful and fancy wares were temptingly displayed. The stall-holders were :

FANCY STALL : Mrs. Pollock-Hill, Mrs. Gorton, Nlrs. Blackwell, Mrs. Royston, Mrs. Haddock, Miss Bond.

CHINA. STILL : Mrs. Buckton. Rumm AGE STALL : Mrs. Young, Mrs. Knowles,Mrs. Turner, Miss Woods, Miss Brimelow.

REFRESHMENT STALL: Miss Jackson assisted by Mrs. Denham, Mrs. Pinnington, Mrs. Osborne, Mrs. Kerfoot, Mrs. Pendlebury, Miss Houghton, Miss Royston, Miss Birchall. _

tOY : Miss D. Denham and Aliss Mary Kerfoot. FIsH POND: Miss Ross.

An interesting feature was the horticultural show and although the entries were small, some creditable specimens were submitted. The awards were as fol- lows :—Table decorations: 1, Miss Hankinson ; 2, Mrs. Haddock ; 3, Miss Minshull. Garden flowers (girls) : 1, May Roberts ; 2, Lucy Hatton. Garden flowers (boys) : 1, Edward Whitlow ; 2 William S. Powell. Buttonhole: I,May Turner; 2;Ellie Turner. Wild flowers (girls) : 1, Lucy Hatton. Wild flowers (boys) : 1, B- Young; 2, Herbert Higham. Apples: 1, Mr. Kerfoot ; 2, Mr. W. Platt. Potatoes : 1. Mr. A. Clarke; 2, Mr. T. Houghton. Cauliflower: 1, Mr. A. Clarke • 2, Mr. James Webb. Onions : 1, Mr Kerfoot ; 12, Mr. J. Daintith. Celery: 1, Mr. L. Warburton. The table decorations were judged by Mrs Rylands and Mrs. Herbert Hatton, and the other exhibits by Mr. George Mather and Mr. Joseph Bennett. The fête was opened by Mrs. Peter Rylands, of Massey Hall, Thelwall, in the presence of a large gather- ing. The Vicar (Rev. W. Pollock-Hill) presided and cordially welcomed Mrs. Rylands who,notwithstanding the many claims upon her from vatious parts of the district, had so kindly come to open the fête. As a rule the fete was in aid of all kinds of deserving objects in the parish, but, last, year the committee made an exception for a special cause and devoted the whole of the money to one object—namely to the raising of .£OO (they required £lso)towards reducing thE quarterly collections in lieu of pew rents. Those who studied the church balance sheet would see that the church- wardens had to dip into the general fund very largely each year to make up the sum of £4O for pew rents. If they could get suilicient that afternoon ta complete the £l5O they would reduce the pew rents by .£l5 a yelr for ever. That was an object to he striven for with all the exertion possible. He hoped also that they would have a balance to hand over to parochial organisations. In the past they had mide grants to the mothers' meeting, the church house and library, parish magazine, Woolston Sunday school, Niigata school building fund, the churchyard improvement, and the ladies' sewing meeting. The objects claiming support were this manyand varied and therefore he hoped they would raise a large aura. (Applause.) _ _ _ _

Mrs. R_ylands said the objects of the fete must appeal to all. It was most desirable that the income of the living should be permanently fixed and not depend upon fluctuations of pew rents. In addition they needed money for other excellent purposes and she hoped the proceeds would meet all their requirements. It was very essential in raisin., funds for the augmenta- tion of the living that they should do so very soon in order to meet the promised grant and she therefore hoped all would do their bmt to make the fête a great success. (Applause). Mr. George Mather proposed a vote of thanks to Mrs. Rylands, and said there were many calls upon the wardens. One of the first objects which they wished to accomplish was the extensioq and beautifying of the church yard, and the organ also required renovating. Mr. Gorton seconded and the vote was heartily accorded.

Many and varied attractions were provided. The Penketh Tannery Prize Brass Band was in attendance and played capital selections. Punch and Judy enter- tainments were given by Professor Deifra and amused large audiences. The shows were ia charge of Messrs. Osborne, T. Antrobus and Vaughan. A series of competitions aroused great interest, tho gentlemen responsible for the arrangements being Messrs. T. Antrobus, Vaughan, Real iffe, Osborne, Rigbay,Royston, Dunham, Blackwell and Critchley. The winners were —Ladies' washing competition : 1. Mrs. Heath ; 2 Miss Critchley. Gents' washing competition: 1, F. Weston; 2, E. Ratcliffe. Gents' hat trimming competition: 1, M. F. Kite; 2, F. Owen (Earlestovrn). Shooting com- petitions (gentlemen) : 1, H. S. Page; (ladies) : 1, Mrs. Bevan; 2, Mrs. G. Pandlebury. Watch competi- tion : 1, Mrs. Lowe (Risley); 2, Mr. G. H. Short (Padkate) ; 3, Miss Wood (Feirnhead). In the evening the grounds were beautifully illuminated, the work being undertaken by Messrs. Rigbey and W. Burrell. The proceeds of the fête amount3d to over £BO.

MINOR POLICE CASES.

At the Newton Occasional Court on Mond iv William Henry Griffiths was, on the evidence of P.C. Lennie, fined 10s.and costs for having been drunk and disorderly. For a similar offence Arthur Sharpe was fined ss. and costs. Patrick Dowd, collier, Clareme-street, Barles- town, ss. and costs; Henry Hall, Haydock, 2s. 6d. and costs.—On Tuesday, before Mr. T. Ormes, three young men, named John Aspinall, John McDermott and John Broster were charged with sleeping out, and each was sentenced to seven days' imprisonment.

CATHOEJIC YOUNG MEN'S SOCIE A Gymnasium in connection with this Society was opened at its new rooms in Fairclough-street on Thurs- day evening. The President (Ar. Cosgrovo), in opening the room. congratulated the members on the interest they had shown in forming the class, and hoped to see many take adv ultage of it and follow the advice of their instructors, Messrs. J. Muskett and J. Swift. (Applause.) The room has been well fitted up, and a strong cross-country team has b en organized with Mr. J. Slavin, an old cross country runner, as captain.

Rev. E. C. E. Carleton's Son at Kainsack. The suggestion is made in this month's " Holy- Trinity Magazine" that the amount raised for the- Society for the Propagation of the Gospel by church collections and missionary b3le9 should be specially set apart for the perish of Kamsack' in Saskatchewan, in North-West Canada, where the Vicar's son is engaged in a very interesting sphere of work. In the providence of God, Trinity Church and parish is linked by close ties with the little settle- ment of Kamsack, continues the Magazine article, in a way that may serve to quicken our interest in missionary enterprize, and enable us to realize the character of the church's work in those remote districts and the need there is for giving it our Aupport. The Vicar of Kamsack writes most enthusiastically of his work in his new parish, and of the hearty reception that he has met with from the settlers throughout the district. Their response to his efforts are very cheering and encourag- ing, and they give freely according to their means to. meet the expenses incident to the maintenance of divine worship. They are, however, mo Aly new settlers, and cannot be expected to spare much on the church work that requires 'to be done : hence the for outside assistance. The new missionary has been assiduous in visiting his parishioners, of whom he gives a very satisfactory acc )unt. Many of the settlers have their homesteads in very distant and isolated districts, and the visit of the parson is much appreciated, as it is something to relieve the monotony of their 1 ,nely life. At his 8 a.m service (Holy Com- munion) recently, there were some present who had driven eight miles for the purpose.

ATTACKED BY A BULL.

- --- A large crowd at Goosntrgh (Lancashire) Agricultural Show on Thursday, were thrown into panic by a hand- some rel bull, which was being led into the judging ring by Thomas Hayhurst, a farmer. The bull suddenly attacked Hayhurst. butted him n in the stomach, andy carrying him forward on its horns, crashed into the fence surrounding the ring. Other farmers rushed to his help, and the brill was blindfolded and led off the field. Hayhurst only sustained minor injuries.

During the week the demand for women domestics and youths 14-15 kept good, but employment for men remained rather dull, very few being able to report . themselves as having obtained situations. - FOR WILTSHIRE BACON try AsHLres,Sankey-St.-Advt. , Alleged Works Theft. William Sudlow, 52, Dalton-bank, appeared in the dock at the Warrington Borough Court, yesterday, with reference to an alleged theft of two brass dices from Messrs. Rylands Bros. Works. -The Chief Constable said he had been asked by the representa- tive of Messrs. Henry Greenall and Co., solicitors, to apologise for non-attendance, as he had 'been Called to London, and he wished the magistrates to allow the case to be withdrawn. The prisoner had given certain information, which would be of great value to them. -Mr. Steel said the prisoner had given certain infor- mation to the firm and the Chief Constable, and he hoped the magistrates would agree to the course suggested.--The Mayor (Alderman Smethurst) said the magistrates bad decided by a majority of one to allow the case to be withdrawn, Street-Trading after Hours. At the Borough Police Court on Monday, before the Mayor, and other magistrates, 16 youths were charged with trading without a licence or badge. Four of them had not been summoned because they were under 12 years of age, and because it was thought a warning would be sufficient to prevent a repetition of the offence. The twelve boys who had been served . with sommonses were Samuel Dickinscn,l3 •, Micheel Glynn , 12 ; Herbert Jones, 12 ; William Campbell, 12 ; , Thomas Green, 15; William Harrop, 12; John Laff, 13; George Peplow, 14; Arthur Chadwick. 12; John William Makinson, 13; Edward Jones, 13; Patrick McLoughlin, 14. The facts of the case were stated by - P.C. Reddington, front which it appeared that some of the defendants sold newspapers and others matches either in the streets or in public-houses. Some of the lads were also out after the time allowed by law.A.ll the defendants pleaded guilty. The chief constable said there was no excuse for the lads, because if they applied to the Police Office badges mid instructions would he supplied free. These were provided at con- siderable expense to the ratepayers. Over 400 badges were out, but only 70 boys' held licenses. A deputa- tion from' the Newsagents' Society had waited upon him, and the complaint was made that boys selling newspapers were in the habit of standing near their ' shop doors and taking their custom from them. That 'was the more objectionable because the shopkeepers had to pay heavy rates. Therefore the complaint was a reasonable one to make -The Mayor said the I cases were very serious, and the lads would not be , allowed to ,sell newspapers, &c., without a licence. The Bench were determined to put down such trading. Peplow, who did not appear, might have been fined 40s. and costs, or sent to prison or to some other place. Dickenson, Laff and Edward Jones would each be fined Se.; Herbert Jones; Campbell and Green 3s. 6d. each; and the other lads is. each. Bogus Engineer at Warrington. At the Borough Police Court on Tuesday, Arthur I William Talbot, of no settled abode was charged that 1 having been convicted on indictment of a crime -a I previous conviction being then proved against him-he I I unlawfully did,within seven years from the expiration ' , of the last sentence, get his livelihood by dishonest means between August 22nd and 26th. Prisoner pleaded guilty.-The Chief Constable said a charge was rat- ferred against prisoner on Tuesday of obtaining food and lodgings by false pretences. the police asked for an adjournment, in order that inquiries might be made with the result that that morning the charge as above preferred. Prisoner arrived in Warrington on August 22nd. He went to Mrs. Hughes, 16, Thewlis- street. and made a statement to her to the effect that 'he had just left his ship, Scots Laddie, in Liverpool, -and required lodgings for six or eight weeks. He said he was employed as third engineer on the boat, and that the sum of £1.27 was due to him from the 'Board of Trade for wages. Time after time he made numerous excuses and promises to the landlady. The woman became suspicious,and informed Superintendent Monaghan, who went- to the house, arrested him, and charged him with obtaining food and lodgings by false pretences. Priaoner bad a long list of convic- tions against him. He started his career in 1889, when he was sentenced to three months' hard labour at Hastings for stealing money, and had served various terms of imprisonment and penal servitude for theft and false pretences. In October, 1906, just eleven months ago. he was sentenced to twelve months' hard labour at Rye for false pretences. He hoped the Bench would give him the full term of imprisonment, namely 12 months.-Prisoner had nothing to say.- Mr. J. Rymer Young (chairman), said they were bound to recognize the importance of his wrong-doing and he would have to go back to gaol for twelve 1 months with hard labour.
